COUNTY PALATINE OF CHESTER. LCCOMO FIVES ACT, 1898. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the Cheshire County Council intend, at the expiration of one calendar month from the date of tbs first publication of this Notice, to apply to the Local Government Board for the confirmation of the following BYE-LAW made by tbem (pursuant to Section 6 of the Locomo- tives Act, 1898) at a meeting of the Council held at Chester Castle, on Thursday, the 13th day of February, 1913, for regulating the' use of Locomotives and of Wagons drawn by Lccomotives on any highway, viz. : A person in charge of a locomotive drawing two or more loaded or unloaded waggons shall not cause or suffer the locomotive to travel on any highway without having (1) a cord or other efficient means of communi- cation extending from the rearmost waggon to such locomotive, and (2) a person who shall (except during the time it is necessary for him to leave his position in order to comply with any statutory regulation or bye- law relating to the use of locomotives on highways) travel in the rear of such waggons. Such last mentioned person shall signal to the driver of such locomotive to stop or drive to the side of the highway when it shall be necessary to do so in order to enable overtaking pedestrians, vehicles, horses,or cattle to pass by. And the person in charge of such locomotive shall, upon receiving any such signal as aforesaid, immediately stop such locomotive and waggons,or draw it and them to the side of highway as the case may require. Provided always that nothing herein contained shall apply to a steam roller not drawing more than three loaded or unloaded waggons. AND NOTICE IS HEREBY FURTHER GIVEN that a copy of such Bye-law will be kept at the Office ta Chester of the undersigned, the Clark to such Council, and the same can be inspected by any of the Ratepayers of the Comity, without fee or reward, any day during office hours for one c,alender month from the date of the first publication of this Notice. AND NOTICE IS HEREBY ALSO FURTHER GIVEN, that copies of the said Bye-law may be obtained at the aforesaid Office by any Ratepayer of the County, on payment of Sixpence for every such copy. Dated this 14th day of February, 1913. • REGINALD POTTS, Clerk to the Cheshire County Council. County °faces, Northgate at., Cheater.

CARTER & SON, BRIDGE ST. WARRINGTON.

FARMERS WAKE UP!

Once we can get the farmers acquiring sufficient c ,n- -fidence in the use of incubators, so soon shall we be able to purchase spring chickens at a -reasonable price. In the production of stock; in the production of agricultura. produce ; the British farmer easily stands first. But I am sorry to say that on the question of poultry raising he is still far behind. The incubator rightly understood and more frequently used will bring about more quickly than anything a reduction in our National Egg Bill. Remember it is still over £8,000,000 par annum!

===== 0002601_19130222_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
PRACTICAL POULTRY FARMING.

[BY REDCAP.] Let us humbly copy nature, From her gifts so manifold, She has given to every creature A chance to imitate the mould. AR 1 IFICIAL INCUBATION. It is two years since I wrote the above words and the continued development of the poultry industry seems to verify more than ever, the practice of al tifi- cial incubation. The enormous demand for early batched chickens could never be coped with by purely natural methods of incubation, hence, the develop- ment of the "wooden hen." Beyond small improve- ments, there is not anything really new in connection with these wonderful appliances. One reason why the English farmer does not make the most of an early spring chicken trade, is because he will not take the trouble to understand Cre incubator. He merely relie, on•old-fashioned methods and consequently his pro- duce is only to hand late on in the season ; of course, marketable chickens, at any period are always profit- able. But there is absolutely no reason why one should not cater for the early spring market by making use of an incubator. These wonderful machines have also been the means of revolutionizing the

DAY OLD CHICKEN" TIINDE. There are those who solely rely on this branch of poultry farming for a livelihood, and I am not sure if this is not after all, the most profit- able branch of poultry farming. It is a well known fact that chicks do not require any food during the first 24 hours of their existence; thus the risk of long railway journeys is very much obviated. This trade could not be carried on if it were not for the incubator. You must remember that a one hundred egg machine will turn out more chicks than half a dozen bloody hens. Then again it is far easier and far more economical to pay attention to one good incubator than it is to look after six broody hens. Then, too, it costs more in carriage to forward lots of chicks and broodies, than it does to merely transport chicks alone The actual cost of producing one hundred chicks is far less by artificial incubation than it is by natural methods. The cost in oil to run the machine is less than the cost in corn to feed sufficient broodies pro- ducing one hundred chicks. Therefore, you will readily admit the value of an incubator as an essential o the day-old chicken trade.

===== 0002601_19130222_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
THE INCUBATOR.

A good many people are prejudiced against an incubator, first, because it is an artificial method, and secondly, because they think they cannot work such a machine. Let us deal then with the first objection. I am, and shall always be against any artificial methcds which are inhuman. I do not believe in any system, artificial or otherwise, which is not consistent with comfort to the creatures involved. Therefore, I hasten to remove any prejudice which may exist on this side against artificial incubation. If failure is to follow the operating of an incubator, the damage will be done, in almost every case, before the chicks are sufficiently developed to suffer. There are, of course, certain precautions to observe before anyone would recommend the use of such a machine. For instance, never use an incubator of an inferior make. Never use a second-hand one unless you have practical knowledge of its real value. There is one redeeming feature about an incubator which is often overlooked when comparing artificial and natural methods of incubation—that is cleanliness. Very often because of unclean surroundings—newly hatched chicks are infested with vermin from a broody hen and this could not happen in the case of an incubator However careful one may be it is most difficult to keep chickens free from vermin when hatched and brooded under natural conditions. On the Other hand chickens hatched in an incubator and reared and brooded in a foster-mother are very rarely troubled in this direction.

THE BROODY HEN. The st,. ry of natural versus artificial incubation is a long one but nevertheless, a most interesting and highly instructive one. It may seem scinewhat para- doxical to say that setting a broody hen on other than her own eggs, is in a sense artificial. But it is so Natural incubation, rightly understood, is the fact of a hen laying a certain number of eggs and sitting on those (her own eggs) for the natural period. his possible for a hen to become broody without having laid a single egg during any current season. In point of fact, I have a Silkie hen that is broody now, and to my own personal knowledge she has not laid an egg for the last seven months Why she is broody I cannot tell you. Therefore, if I were to put eggs under this hen, and practically compel her to hatch those egg by placing some protection around her, I claim that in a sense I am hatching by an artificial system. These illustrations are simply being used to show that if it is right to compel incubation in one way, it is surely right in another way, and inasmuch as the incubator system is the most hygienic the balance is in favour of the incubator. So much then for prejudice !

3IANIITLATI ON.

The second objection was inability to work an incubator. This is a very simple matter when one _uses just the ordinary amount of commonsense consistent with a strict following out of general directions. There are so many different makers of incubators and so many different types that it is impossible to lay down, in a strict sense, the exact method of manipulation. And beyond a few essential rules it is always best and quite safe to follow the directions sent out with each machine. The following points, however, should always be observed. An incubator should be placed in a room that is not often frequented. It should have a floor of solid earth or stone, so that as little vibration is caused as possible. It danuld also be perfectly free from draughts, of even temperature, and with a perfect system or ventilation. Plenty of fresh air is an essential.

===== 0002601_19130222_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
TEMPERATURE.

It does not much matter what the temperature of the room may be, providing it is kept something like even. The following scale will doubtless be useful. I am indebted to Messrs Sprhtt for the help given at this point. They say that When the room stands at 50, 60, 70, 80', keep the incubator drawer 1_ 105' 104', 103', 102 ,° as nearly as you can at )

The eggs of waterfowl may be batched about 20 belnw the corresponding ones given for hens' eggs in the table.

===== 0002601_19130222_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BEST INCUBATORS.

Nothing has been more remarkable in the poultry industry during recent years than the exceptional growth of artificial incubation. The number of people who now possess incubators is truly astonishing, and it may be taken for granted if these people did not obtain excellent results they would quickly discard them and revert to natural methods. Still, it should not be supposed that every machine advertised irrespective of cost is one that will prove useful, and bring forth good chickens every three weeks. On the contrary, there are quite a lot of machines now adver- tised admittedly low in price, but so faulty in con- struction, and built on such flimsy lines, that it is impossible for them to keep in sound condition very long. Although they may produce chickens for perhaps for six months, after the first year's use they are not to be relied on ; the wood commences to shrink, 15ossibly the tank begins to leak, and the regulating appliances do not work finely. It is by . _

fai: the clieap-e;t to go in for a sound machine to start with, one that has been made by a firm of repute, and that can bA depended on. The most popular size at the present day seems to be the 100-egg machine; this can be had of a very good make for about and the money will be well spent.—From "Farm and Home."

A layman like himself would not have dared to make sucha suggestion. Personally, he felt the committee were bound to act on the advice of the Medical Officer • they had no option in the matter. They had two small schools containing not much more than a handful of scholars, and they were faced with the difficulty of the displace- ment of one of thern.What would happen to the teachers if one school were closed ? They could not make places even for teachers, and if Cairo-street School was closed the teachers, unless placn could be fcund for them in other schools, must lose their occupation. He might go further and say that if it were found necessary to reduce the school staff the committee would make the most strenuous efforts to find accommodation for the displaced teachers in some other schools. The com- mittee had always thoughtfully and kindly dealt with teachers in the difficult position of being in danger of losing their places owing to a reduction in the number of scholars in certain schopls. He did not anticipate for a moment that they would depart from that policy which had been adopted hitherto. The CHAIRMAN : I shall be glad if the amendment is not pressed. He had not the slightest fear but that the new infant department would be to all intents and purposes in everything but name a Council school. The terms of the teachers' engagements, the conditions of teaching of children, and the permanence of arrangements in regard to the teachers and their employment were all secured under the agreement. As a fairly representa- tive Nonconformist he had no hesitatiom in asking the committee to accept the scheme. (Hear, hear). They had an interview with the head master of the Secondary School upon the matter, who reported that the time table already provided for one and a half hours per week to he devoted to a religious service at the opening of each morning session, and that it would be difficult to devote any more time to this purpose without overloading the time for secular instruction. , HOCKEY.

LATCHFORD.

The First Eleven defeated Bowdon College on Satur- day by 5-3. The initial stages of the game saw the home team to advantage and they led 4-1 at half time, but the visitors were a very different team afterwards 4nd scoring twice they seemed likely to draw but Latchford put on another goal and won with a nice margin. The College men (who have the advantage of daily practice) exhibited all the nice points of hockey and the passing among the forwards was well-planned and required some stopping. Fortunately for Latchford Pletcher at right-back was in his best form and removed the ball from many ominous positions. Wilkinson was the best of the halves. The College goalkeeper showed great resource andi agility and kept out some warm shots. Pearson 3 • Travis 1; Minards, 1 ; were the scorers for Latchfocl. The Second Eleven were beaten 7-1 at Brooklands but in justice to them it must be stated that five of the regular team were off through injuries, &a. Up to the interval Latchford had a full share of attacking and were only losing 1-0 then, but on turning round the Brooklands forwards were irresistible and got through 6 times, Willing at outside left playing very finely and scoring four goals. Newbold scored the only goal for Latchford and played quite well on his first.appearance. Eccles, T Banks, Fletcher and Gibbs also worked hard against a strong attack. The game was most enjoyable and the hockey clean on both sides. To-day the First Eleven at Knutsford v Mid-Cheshire is :—J Bruhl, goal ; W Fletcher, C Billington, backs; J B Wilkinson, A E Bradshaw, A Knight, halves; R A Dale, J E Pearson, W H Minards, A Starkey, E Banks, forwards ; referee, Mr Cobb. Bus leave the Academy at 1.30. ALL-NIGHT CARS.

Support to the proposal that Manchester and Salford cars should run all night for the benefit of night workers was given at a meeting of the Manchester and Salford Trade and Labour Council on Wednesday night. RECLUSE STARVED TO DEATH.

Emma stamp, a widow, aged 60, was found dead in her house at Lympstone, Devon, in a frightfully neglected condition. She lived alone, and although she had money in the bank, she had died from starvation.

===== 0002601_19130222_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
THE O'MARA OPERA CO. AT WARRINGTON.

MR. JOSEPH O'MARA as Don Jose in "Carmen."

The O'Mara Grand Opera Company are due at the Royal Court Theatre next week. On Monday evening" Faust" will be presented, on Tuesday "Carmen," on Wednesday" II Trovatore "(afternoon) and " Maritana " (evening). The company is an unusually capable one. In all there are a dozen principals, while the chorus i 3 about 80 strong, and a number of instrumentalists travel with the company to augment the local orchestra. Lovers of grand opera should not miss the opportunity of hearing the company next week. The company includes Mr Joseph O'Mara and Miss Edith Evans. The latter is a very gifted singer with a voice of great power and flexibility ; she has appeared in grand opera at Covent Gardens.

"THE SIN OF PLEASURE."

Rev. A. M. Mitchell's Indictment of the Church.

The Rev A M Mitchell, vicar of Burtonwood, has a characteristic arlicle in the current issue of his prirish magazine, headed "The sin of pleasure." He writes: —A Warrington varson has bad a good deal to say latterly about the present-day craze for pleasure. No doubt there is a great deal of truth in what this gentleman and others say, for, apparently, there is more desire, in some parts of the country anyhow, for pleasure than for religi,n, more in attendance at picture shows than at the churches. The twentieth century, still quite young, is more given to pleasure than its predecessor. This does not necessarily mean that this present generation isless godly than any of its forerunners; the vice of the picturedrome and other places of amusement which prove so irresistibly attractive does not, of necessity, stand for "the sin of pleasure." Non-church-going is not the equivalent of irreligion nor is pleasure synonymous with godlessness. We may deplore the extensive patronage bestowed upon these new pleasure institutions, and the apparent growing unpopularity of religious services, in the case of those who unduly frequent such, but these are not infallible signs of sin and sinfulness. They are, rather, signs of a very natural, innate, human desire for realisation in some degree of that which is the right of every man, woman and child—a share, a fair share, of the joy of life. What all the world's a-seeking is this joy of life but, unfortunately, the seeking" isn't always in the wisest way nor sought for in those quarters where true joy is to be found. The fact is, we are in a transition time, an old order of pleasure is ghanging, giving place to a new in which and 4hrough which, unquestionably, God will fulfil Himself as He has done in so many other ways.

Every allowance should be made for the deadly dull existence, for the drab conditions of life under which so very many of our brave, uncomplaining toilers are forced to pass their days. Why are such men, with their belongings, forced as is so generally the case, to live like beasts? They are compelled from birth, though no fault of their own, to a hideous environment hitherto deemed fit, quite good enough, and in a Christian country too, for the ordinary industrial labourer and " the likes o' him." Any place of amuse- ment, any drinking-house, any a arm comfortable,bright environment in the evening time is as a baven of refuge from the slum conditions to which these unfortunate men and their belongings are consigned on their appear- ance in this world. We incline to think the Church should lay the blame at home,should begin with herself and not the erring pleasure-seekers, the victims of a

social system for which they, in no wise, are re.ponsible. Why has the Church—in its widest and truest sense —suffered the poorer sort to exist—it is not living— under such depressing,degr.ding, destructive conditions of life? Why did not the Church insist long, long ago, upon decent, human-like housing conditions for these indus- trial workers who constitute the back-bone of our natior al strength, and are the fovs et oriae Of our national wealth ? A considerable portion of the life of these men is spent in dangerous toil, in manual labour of a kind which sows with unerring certainty physical disease for which there is no compensation hut which, only too surely,shortens the natural span of human life, for the greater number. Would it not be wiser, would it not be more consistent, would it not be more charit- able, to condemn a social system productive of poverty and destitution, of disease and deeth, of drink and dirt, then to take to task victims of it because they seek relief in forms of pleasure which are not the highest, and which did not elevate the spirit as, we submit, all true pleasure should ?

WOMEN WORKERS.

What the Vote would do.

Many people would be in favour of women's suffrage if they really thought it would help women to improve the conditions of their work. If they would look at the history of the franchise for working men, they would find their answer. Men have steadily used their votes to get better condi- tions as workers. Instances of their success are many, among the most notable being the legislation of Trades Unions, the Truck Act, the various laws regulating work in factories, the appointment of factory inspectors and many others. All these have been passed in the interest of the workers, and some of them have helped women as well as men. But where women have themselves the right to vote, they have taken further steps to. protect thymselves.

- In our Country the most sweated workers. are women. In New Zealand and Australia sweating has been abolished. The women of New Zealand were enfranchised in 1893, in 1894 a law was passed which, gradually amended and improved, has resulted in the. disappearance of sweating, though before 1893 there were cases of it just as bad as there are here. In Australia the beginnings of reform were made before women had the vote, but the thoroughness an&completeness of the work has followed, and not preceded women's enfranchisement. In two of the six Suffrage States of Ameria, there is an Eight Hours day for women, and in one there is a nine Hours day. In none of the States of America where women are disfranchised is there an Eight Hours law, and in very few is there any restriction at all. The person who wears the shoe knows best where it pinches, and women want to be able to give their own opinion about laws regulating their work. Even Anti-Suffragists have to admit (" Anti-Suffrage Handbook," page 42.) "Women are limited to 45 hours a week in New Zealand, except in woollen mills, when 48 hours is allowed; and in Australia 48 hours a week. In this country the limitation of hours for women is—" In textile factories, 561/2 hours net ; in non- textile factories, 60 hours net." It is a pity that this handVook ig only circulated, privately; the Anti-Suffragists would no doubt be afraid to scatter this statement brdadcast as a leaflet.

E. L. B.

COST OF EDUCATION

Increase In the Estimates of the Lancashire

Authority.

The Lancashire County Education Committee On Monday approved the estimates for the ensuing year. Elementary education, it is estimated will cost £551,950 against £535,160 for the previous year, and higher education is put down at £112,975 as compared with £108,725. _

-Mr Cocksbott explained that the increase on the elementary side is due to the automatic increase of teachers' salaries, a larger number of special subject teachers, renewal of worn out furniture which was handed over by the voluntary school managers. increased rates on the school buildings, more doctors and nurses for rredical inspection. Mr James Walker explained that the advance in the higher education estimates was because non-county k ()roughs and urban and rural districts have bigger demands on their secondary schools, scholarships are applied to a longer school life, and two grammar schools are to have a sistance.

Report .W.;;,,i";,i; that the Local Government Board has authorised a rate of 2d. for higher education Formerly the rate was limited to 2d.

The best team lo,t at Wakefield on Saturday. Two minutes from time Warrington were on level terms with the Trinity men, and deserved at least a division of the points. There was just time however, for one of the home threequarters to sprint over at the corner while the Warrington men appealed for an obvious informal;ty. Jolley might have tackled the man, but .be appeared to wait for the whistle, with the result that Wakefield won quite undeservedly in the last minute. How often, I wonder have the Warrington men been told to Ray to the whistle, and not imagine that their own interpretation of the rules will be accepted by the Referee. The match was thrown away after some excellent, persevering football, had produced 12 points. We have the satisfaction only of knowing that the Warrington men gave a good account of themselves on Yorkshire soil. They are due " over the border" again ere long.

The forwards again played dashing football and gave the men behind plenty of opporiunity. In the second half, at one time, Warrington had only three men in the pack owing to injuries, aud Chesters was absent nearly all this half. Thomas and Fearnley were leaders in many a raid, and the former kicked three capital goals. Tranter again distinguished himself with a solo try, as against Leeds, and in the latter half of the game Brooks played fine football on the wing.

At half back Nicholas and Brown did not combine effectively, for the simple reason that Brown had to take the outside berth, which was unsuited to his style. The two men ought to have changed places, but instead Brown goes back to the " A " team to-day, and Daintith is re-introduced, a move which seems to have very little to recommend it. Daintith's limits are known, but there are possibilities in Brown yet undeveloped. Half back is likely to be the chief difficulty in the cup-ties, and in my opinion the " A " team junior should be persevered with. Nicholas has played outside half before!

Wakefield scored five tries to Warrington's three goals two tries which seems to suggest superiority, but as already said Warrington were the better team all round. The weak spot was Rushton, who quite failed to justify his selection to the centre position. He handled the ball right enough in combination, but on the defence he was a nonentity. Poymon, the centre • opposing him made so light of Rushton's tackling that he ran in three tries! This would not have happened with Renwick or Dickenson at centre. You must have centres who can spot their man and bold him. Warrington's cup-tie successes have all been gained through impregnable defence allied to a reasonably smart attack. A weak mat, on the defence makes all the difference between winning and losing, as it did on Saturuay.

Warrington's Hard Luck at Wakefield.

The Wirepullers were at Wakefield on Saturday, the match being set aport for the benefit of Slater and Walton, two of the most noted Trinity players. Though the day was fine and the conditions excellent, there were only about 3,000 spectators present to witness a same which was full of interest right to the close. Neither of the beneficiares was able to play, owing to injury, bet the teams were representative, and lined out. as follows: _ _ _

_ Warrington—Jolley, back ; Brookes, Rushton,Tranter and Davies, threequarter-backs ; Brown and Nicholas. half-backs ; G Thomas, Chesters, Fearnley, Cox, R Thomas and Naylor, forwards. Wakefield Trinity—Smith, back ; Bennett, Lynch, Poynton and Mills, threequarter-hacks ; Newhould and Smales, half-backs : Crosland,G Taylor,Willis,Kershaw, Johnson and Anton, forwards.

Referee, Mr R H Jackson, St Helens. Warrington started well, and smart handling and running by both halves and tbreequarters soon had Smith, the borne custodian, in difficulties. He was none too safe under pressure, and the Trinitarians were relieved when Lynch secured the ball and cleared out the invaders. Strong forward play by the home men resulted in Warrington being driven back. The Trinity men got possession and were passing near midfield when 'neuter dashedlin and snapped,up the ball. The lengthy Warrington centre was soon off at full speed, and after a great run he scored in good position for Thomas to add a goal. The reverse roused the locils who attacked hotly, but Jolley, Tranter and Nicholas defended well. Another fine run by Tranter and a particularly good effort by Brookes gave Warrington another look in, but this time Smith was not to be , caught napping and he forced matters at the opposite end. Wakefield got to the Warrington quarters as the result of good passing, but when in the danger zone the movements generally broke down at Mills. Eventually Poynton made the best of a chance which came as a result of Newbould's good work, forcing his way over at the corner. Taylor missed the goal shot. Warrington were the dangerous set from the drop-out and Tranter got well into his stride again, and, passing to Cheaters, the forward looked a likely candidate for a score, when he was overhauled by Bennett. This was hard luck for the " Wirepullers" for the advantageous psition had been gained by splendid work. Wakefield worked their way into the visiting quarters, and Jolley failing to respond to a huge kick from Lynch, a round of passing between Bennett, Anton and Poynton resulted in the last-named going over for an uncon- verted try. Interval : Wakefield Trinity, two tries (six poalts) ; Wurringten, one goal one try (five k points).

The second hill' was entered upon with vigour, both I sides playing good footbal.. For some time it was a battle royal between the forwards. Eventually Mills got away for the home lot, but be did not travel far, and superior combination between Brown, Nicholas and Rushton eased the tension somewhat. Following up a tall kick by Newhould, Johnson was credited with a try for Wakefield, and immediately after Poynton recorded his third try of the afternoon. The visitors were now seven points in arrears and they showed any amount of dash, the forwards forcing matters splendidly. From a difficult position George Thomas dropped a beautiful goal. Cheaters was off hurt, but despite this drawback the visitors continued to have the better of the deal. A round of passing by the Wakefield backs terminated in fault; fashion. and Fearnley fook full advantage of the mistake to sucre. George Thomas kicked the goal, so that the scores were now equal. There were only a couple of minutes to play, but Mills got hold from a pass by Lynch and scored at the corner, though there was a general suspicion that the player knocked the ball forward. G Taylor again missed the gosh Result : Wakefield Trinity, five tries (15 points) ; Warrington. three goals two tries (12 points).

FRIENDLY MATCH. WARRINGTON " A " v FLIXION.

The appearance of new talent, in the " A " team last Saturday against Fliaton .eaused a good crowd to assemble at Wilderpool. Two ex-livertoniana in Berry and liardsley made their debut before a Warrington audience whilst a trial was given to a new half back named Morgan. The start was 'atomising for the home side, for only a few minutes bad elapsed when Blundell scored a try,after Bradshaw had executed a fine dribble, which Berry improved. Warrington were always dangerous, and after Berry had made a fine attempt to crotA, a pa4slng-mdVetlient lbd up to Daintoth making

an opening for Bardsley, the wing man dashing over in clear style. Berry again added the goal. Flixton rallied splendidly and several smart runs endangered Brown's change. The custodian cleared effectively, and Clare, on the right, made several futile attempts to break down the defence. It was not until close on the interval that a further score came, and this time Gibbs put on the finishing touch after Bradshaw had ably led a forward dribble. At the interval Warrington led by three goals, three tries (15 points) to Fliston nil. Turning round, Bardsley was well to the fore with a strong run and was grassed only inches short from the line. loc,r a time Mixton held the opposition at bay. but eventually the home forwards controlled the picks better, and after Daint ith had opened out the game Bardsley succeeded in scoring. Clare proved very elusive and crossed inear the corner on the right. The pressure was maintained. Arnold was given possession and he forced his way over the line. Flixton took advantage of some loose play by the borne backs and Burgess crossed in a good position, but the attempt to improve was a weak one. In the closing stages Flixton fell away and tries for Warrington were scored in rap'd succession by Clare and Jordan,Berry placing one goal front an extreme angle. Itr.sult : Warrington,four goals eight tries (32 points) ; Flixton, one tr; (three points).

===== 0002601_19130222_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
After the match the Warrington Athletic Club chimpionship team, which did so well at Lancaster last Saturday, will indulge in a five miles scratch race. C Vose, H Holbrook, and A Greggon are all expected to turn out. A. :collection will be male towards the championship expenses, and I hop there will be a generous response from all admirers of our excellent Athletic Club.

Mr E Osborne, a member of the Warrington and Lancashire County Committees, has had an article in the " Daily Mail" this week on the need for reorganisation in the Northern Union. This seems to be a pet topic just now and, personally, I have seen few suggestions which are either new or valuable. My friend, Mr Osborne, has ideas of his own which will, no doubt, provoke plenty of criticism. For the benefit of readers of this column who may not have seen the " Daily Mail" I print a few of extracts from Mr Osborne's article, which embody his main proposals. He says : And so members of committees are beginning to see that the gaol e of football should be a pastime and not it money-making machine. That object can only be attained by a thorough reorganisation of the preeent system of selecting players and in the payment to them for services renuered. The sport must come first) payment for placing must be a secondary consideration. To achieve that very desirable end 10061 talent will have to be encouraged. Equal wages must be paid to the players—no man mnst receive more than another. To secure that .combination in a team which is absolutely essential the custom of crediting goals or tries to an individual will have to be stopped. The habit fosters selfishness and produces Individualism. It has always seemed to me silly to credit, in an annual table, the man who scores it try with three points that have been most probably earned by the previous passing movement of three or four CI his colleagues. I have no doubt that a thorrpugh system of gradation would be successful. Ihe game must be commenced at school. Some clubs provide the necessary clothing and boots to the schoolboys, and the goods remain in the custody of a teacher. The next grade should be for boys who have left school—say for those between the ages of fourteen and seventeen. Every club, I think, which can afford it ought to run a third team, and poaching of players should not be allowed, change of residence only being permissible for transfer to another town's team. The importation of players has not been a success. It has caused jealousy among .the local players, and has resulted in the best not tetng got out them. It has created a bad impression among the sport-loving community, and has been the means of largely destroying the nobler side of the game. Team-building is not easy, and it can be made lighter by refusing to squander club funds on ready-made players and more effective and endurable by the encouragement and training of one's own townsmen from their youth up. Local patriotism and a real interest in athletics make a combination that goes n long way towards carrying a town club to creditable success. They would give us, too, citizens who, when their playing days are over, would take a lasting pride in their achievements, and v‘ould take their places in the world respected by all. They would certainly not be, as vce repro' to see some nowadays, a reflection upon the fair name of such a popular sport.

PLATFORM TICKETS FOR L & N.-W. RAILWAY STATIONS.

A great cause or inconvenience to the travelling public, is the undesired frequenter of railway station platforms. In order that platforms shall he used only by legitimate travellers and friends, the L. & N.-W. Railway Co. will inaugurate a ssstem of issuing plat- form tickets to non-travellers at a penny each com- mencing on the 21th February at Warrington Rank Quay Station. The automatic ticket machine, which this Company will place in use, although a familiar feature on the Continent, has only recently been intro- duced into this country ; but signs are not wanting that in the near future it will form part of the equipment of the more important of our Railway Stations. There is no doubt that this arrangement will be welcomed by the general public, especially at those stations where one is confronted with the sign " Passengers only are allowed on the platform." The charge of one penny, although not a larre sum, has been found by experience to act as a sufficient deterrent to those persons who have no particular business on the platform. whilst inflicting no hardship upon those who wish to see their friends off by train. Machines have already been installed at Wolverhampton and at Wellington (Salop), and in each case have been found to work very satisfactorily. Those persons residing in the town who are subscribers to Messrs Wyman's Library may be allowed through to the ookstall on the production of their library tickets.

The Exati iner Saturday February 22, 1913,

ph to, F. Mounfield A. GREGSON,

of the Warrington Athletic Club, who won the West Lancashire Junior Championship last Saturday at Lancaster.

THE IMPS IN A FLOOD.

CROSS COUNTRY RUNNING.

Warrington Wins Four Championships in

One Day.

The local athletic Club achieved remarlable successes by winning the individual and team honours in both the Junior and Senior Championships of the Wee, Lancashire Association at Lancaster on Saturday last, end In doing so they established an unsurpassable record by "cleaning up" the division as follows : December 14th, 1912, at Warrington, Joseph Hanson won the individnal Novice Uhampionship, and J Nurse, H Woods and W Walters were the other counters who helped Hanson to complete a double event by winning the Novice team championship of West Lancashire. January 18th, at St Helers, Nelson Stafford won the individual honour and with Jeffrey Wataln, G Wakefield and J Thornily formed the" new team " which accom- plished a unique club performance by winning, within such a short petiod, another double event in the novice championship of the St Helens Association. February 15th. at Scale Hall Farm, Lancaster, rhris Vos_e emphasised the short comings of the Olympic Selection Committee by the runaway fashion in which he won the divisional senior championship and with H Holbrook, W Bolton, F Antrobus, J Parsonage, and F Shadwell completel a double event by winning the team honours. Alfred Gregsin won the Junior Championship, and had for his nearest attendant .1 Hanson, who together with J Nurse, J Watson, N Stafford and W Walters, formed the country six for team honours and completed the second double event on the same day. From all quarters congratulations have been showered upon the runners and officials and such grand results muot be very gratifying to all local sportsmen, particu- larly to those who support the management during times of financial pressure.

RYLANDS RECS v LEESFIELD TRINITY.

This friendly match took place last Saturday on the Gorsey-lane enclosure before a fair attendance, Lees- field, who came down on a guarantee, arriving half-an- hour late. Ryland:: were without Carter, Floyd, Stretford and Horrocks. The visitors kicking off, Bramhall, ,ho was playing centre, returned the ball to the visitors' quarters. Both sides were playing pretty football, and atter a series of scrummages the home backs got going, which ended in Barton scoring at the corner. Leah failed at goal. From a pass by Leah, Griffiths scored, Morris converting. The visitors next had a spell of attacking from ,hich they scored, half- time arriving with the score Itylands 2 goals 4 tries to Leesfield 2 tries. The second half was more even, one of the visiting backs intercepting a pass. raced past all opposition and scored a try froni half-way. This rowed the wire men and Leah and Griffiths passing and re-passing, Griffiths put the finishing touch to a grand effort, the goal kick failing, the game finishing in favour of the home team by 2 goals 5 tries (19 points) to 3 tries (9 points), after a peasant game to watch from a spectator's point of view.

'LYMNI V LATCH FORD.

There was a large crowd to witness this second round tie on the ground of the former. The visitors were the first to score. Lyrnm were avarded a free kick just outside the penalty arei. Hinde took a kick and evalized with a capital drive. THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY FEBRUARY 22, 1913.

Those who are in FRANCE AND favour of "spreading NATIONAL the military spirit in SERVICE. England," and of making a final depar- ture from that non-interventionist policy which has so successfully kept us out of European quarrels, will read with pleasure what a, prominent French news- paper has to say about LORD ROBERTS, campaign in favour of conscription,which itis still thought advisable to describe as national service. The idea that the campaign is being carried on in behalf of home defence is set aside with the most complete nonchalance The, inestimable advantage that would follow from conscription in England, says the military correspondent of the Echo de Paris, would be to drag the country from the lethargy which it owes to its insular position. England would prove itself to be awake to the European role. There would be no longer a hesitating attitude in favour of European crises. Carefully elabo- rated contracts would be entered into with Continental Powers—France, for exam plc— and there can hardly be a war in any part of the world in which England would not have bound itself to play a part. We have not quoted the French newspaper, but have only interpreted what we believe to be its real meaning. Without being in the least degree too frank, the Echo de Paris shows us what Lord ROBERTS'S campaign means to our French friends. They regard it as being undertaken in their behalf rather than our own.

It is remarked that in his recent speech at Bristol Lord ROBERTS explained that without recourse to compulsory military service England cannot accomplish the double role that is imposed upon her. And what is this double role? It is to send an expeditionary corps to help her friends, and at the same -- Sir Henry Hibbert had time ensure the home territory against any A POLICY OF POST- one disadvantage, as a possible raid on the part of the enemy. This, DATED CHEQUES. TOI y candidate, for it need not be pointed out, drags us far away fighting the Chorlor from any policy that English statesmen of division. The idea is that we should raise an Opposition are falling victims to a kind of army to fight in a quarrel in which we have mania for pledging themselves to do nothing ro share, and that, in fact, we should bind until after two elections. It is the Nemesis ourselves to fight for France against of unfair tactics. They have tried to fix a charge of bad faith upon the Government Germany in any quariel that France herself because in 1906 the Liberal Government was might consider it to be for her honour or her pledged not to introduce a Home Rule Bill in advantage to bring about. The of bad faith, are now gradually hampering entente cordiale is a security for peace ; a themselves with a vast accumulation of military alliance would almost certainly be post-dated cheques. the signal for European conflict. Even Lord ROBERTS himself should be warned by the interpretation which is put upon his words in France. Our insular posi- tion has up to now been considered to be worth something to us. To be " ringed round by the inviolate sea" has been regarded by Englishmen for centuries past as a special blessing of Providence. The sea has not only kept us in a grand security; but it has saved us the expense of costly armies, it has enabled us to use in industry that national brain and vigour and muscle which would otherwise have .been expended in drill. Above all, it has enabled us to develop to the full our natural aptitude for commerce and manufacture. We in these little islands have led Europe in what is most essential to national. well-being. The Parisian newspaper to which we have made reference speaks of our insular position as producing national lethargy. Perhaps it does, On that point we are open to convic- tion ; but our present opinion is that in all essential matters we are rather wide awake, and that even in international affairs,. as has recently been proved in connection with the East of Europe, we are enormously more powerful and influential than we could be if we were included in one of the military groups. Lord ROBERTS has no reason to feel grateful to his French exponent. There is not the least inclination to raise an army in in this country for the mere purpose of back- ing up any European power whatever.

what they themselves would give Ulster if the power was in their hands. There is only one dignified, statesmanlike course open to them, and that is to come in with the Liberal majority and share to some extent the triumph which will be all the more glorious through being a generation overdue!

Warrington must MR. IRIRRELL AT surely come under the WARRINGTON. "favoured nation "

clause, in regard to the eminence of its political visitors. In recent years we have had upon the Liberal platform such shining stars lin the political firmament as Mr. Lloyd George, Mr. Winston Churchill Lord Haldane, Mr Sydney Buxton,Col. Seely, and Mr. Birrell, the latter of whom has been so kind as to visit us twice. Even the opposition platform has not been devoid of ornament, though the Tory " Cabinet " contains but few articles .of vertu in comparison with the choice display in the Liberal household. No doubt we, in Warrington, owe a good deal to the good offices of Mr. Crosfield, and his influence in politically high places, for the intellectual treats provided by such men as Mr. Birrell and his fellow-Ministers who have addressed the electors of Warrington from time to time. On Saturday evening last a crowded audience in Parr Hall was privileged to hear the case for Home Rule stated with a sincerity, logic, and persua- siveness at the command of, perhaps, no other living orator but the present Chief Secretary for Ireland. When Mr. Birrell had finished his exposition of the Bill now passing slowly but surely through the legislative machine, no one present could doubt that the demand of Ireland was not only representa- tive of the national sentiment, but based on the principles of justice and right. No wonder Mr. Crosfield, with an intimate knowledge of the man and his work, should say: "The Irish Secretaryship has been the grave of many a statesman, but that will never be said of our guest of this evening who, I verily believe, is going to share with the late Sir Henry Campbell Bannerman and Mr. Asquith, in one of the proudest triumphs of modern times."

Mr. Birrell naturally THE ULSTER devoted considerable PROBLEM. time to a _searching

analysis of the Ulster problem, and particularly as it affects the still recalcitrant opposition. As a problem pure and simple it bristles much less for- midably since the election in Derry, which proved that the elv ctors in a constituency in the very heart of the revolting (?) province were by no means so averse to Home Rule as the Tory fire-brands had made out. That election dealt a blow to the anarchical exponents of Toryism from which they are still staggering. What are they going to do? —asks Mr Birrell with quiet persistence. If, it is a big "if "—Mr Bonar Law obtained a majority at the next election he would still have to eeal with Home Rule, and Mr Birrell expressed his certainty of conviction that " after the passage of this Bill, no Parliament ever likely to assemble again at Westminster will authorise any Government to suppress the passionate desire of the Irish people to govern themselves." The Opposition has arrived at pretty much the same position with regard to Home Rule, as it has with regard to National Insurance. Bitter invective based on misrepresentations of the most flagrant kind can no longer have any effect on the situation. Threats of civil war in Ulster have become so irresistibly comic that even the throat of Mr. Bonar Law would contract with a spasm of aversion before giving fresh utterance to them to-day. Again we ask, what are he and his party going to do? If they possess any of that elementary reason often called "horse sense" they will accept the inevi- table and join with the Government in settling the Irish question on the lines alone on which any permanent structure of home government can be erected. The Opposition cannot tell us either what Ulster wants or

ELT

1 NOTES AND NOTIONS.

We hear that since the arbitration with Messrs. James Fairclough and Sons, Ltd., the Corporation have come to terms with Mr. Alfred Smith for the purchase of his premises at Market Gate, and with Mr. Wm. Gill for his shop in Bridge-street.

In December last the official German unem- ployment rate was 2.8 per thousand. In the same month the official British rate was five points less (2.3). It should be remembered that the German unemployment rate is ad- mittedly lower than the actual state of things warrants.

One of the most striking features of the British trade returns of the last few years is the growth of our exports of motor-cycles. The annual val-ae of those exports rose from £112,502 in 1910 to £278,856 in 1911, and £526,743 in 1912, doubling each year. Of parts of motor-cycles the exports rose from ,E 37,894 in 1910 to £186,237 in 1912. That is not all the story. The imports of cycles are falling at the same time as the exports are rising, and are only 8 per cent. of our exports. The trade is getting on very . well without, Protection.

* * Councillor Henry Roberts, in a fighting speech at the Parr Hall on Saturday evening, suggested some very pertinent questions which independent electors might put to Mr. Harold Smith, M.P. It will indeed be interesting to hear what the Member has to say of these possibly unpleasant reminders of "what I have said during election exuber- ance." Dues he still regard himself as pledged to vote against food taxes? And what does he think of the Tory conspiracy against Trade Unionism and of the persecu- tion of Roman Catholic workingmen by the Orange fanatics of Belfast? *

That much-discussed old landmark-- Messrs. Fairclough's warehouse at Bridge Foot, which was the subject of important arbitration proceedings last week—is novir almost demolished. We understand that the Street Improvement Committee have decided to pull down the entire block of property between the bridge and Water street. This includes Mr. Morton's shop, several houses, purchased from the Co-operative Society, and other buildings in the rear. Mr. Taylor's property in Water- street has been absolutely cleared away. It is expected that the bridge contractor will be placed in possession of the whole of the site next week.

* * * Widnes just missed having its " Driver Knox" case this week. At Monday's Police Court there an engine-driver was charged with drunkenness on the Saturday night, and a sergeant and constable deposed to his condition, and apprehension by them. During the hearing Councillor Lewis, J.P., left the bench for the witness box, and stated that he had seen the incident of the arrest described by the police, and in his opinion there was no sign of drink on the man. The magistrates dismissed the case on the ground that there was "a conflict of evidence.' Either the police or the engine-driver have suffered an injustice. ,We wonder how far the diplomatic action of the Bench can be traced to the Knox case. * * The objection which Mr. F. R. Roberts raised to the proposed transfer of the Cairo street Infants' (Council) School to a new' department to be foimed at Holy TrinitY (Church) school has been amply justified by subsequent events. Had the scheme been allowed to go through as originally sub- mitted, only the goodwill of the managers could have been invoked to safeguard the interests of teachers and parents in the matter of religious instruction. As the result of Mr. Roberts's timely intervention there is now an arrangement, in black and white, by which only undenominational, teaching shall be given in the new infants' department and none of the members of the transferred staff shall be required to give undenominational instruction. The teachers are thus given security of tenure without any variation of the conditions under which they entered the service of the authority and the rights of Nonconformist children are fully protected.

The Liberals of the Widnes division have secured a real fighting candidate in the per- son of Mr J B Martindale,who created such a good impression by his short, racy and incisive speech at the Parr Hall meeting on Saturday. Mr. Martindale has youth on his side and is in all respects admirably fitted for, the uphill task upon which he has so pluckily embarked. He represents the truly' democratic type of Radicalism which has swept the country of late years and his method of presenting political facts and arguments is most attractive and convincing. On Monday at Widnes the Chairman of the Liberal Association, M. J. B. Aitken, enter- tained the leaders of the division to a dinner at the Liberal club, and the new candidate was introduced. Mr. Martindale's speech 01 that occasion had about it the true ring of progressive advancing Liberalism. Colonel Hall Walker will have to put up a more strenuous fight than is his custom or he will assuredly find himself unhorsed by this keen opponent, who while he hits fairly also hits in straight and unflinching fashion.

Several of our railway companies are borrowing good ideas from America. The London and North-Western and the Great Western are adopting the automatic ticket machine for admission to station platforms, and after Monday next, Warrington people 'who wish to accompany friends to the plat- forms at Bank Quay Station will have to put a penny in the slot first. The companies foresee both improved station facilities and a .considerable revenue in the idea. The public Pays, as usual!

* * Is it not high time that the circumlocu- tion methods of the modern Arbitration Court gave place to some simpler and less expensive plan of acquiring property required for public improvements? The principle einbodied.in the latest Acts of Parliament on More ambitious undertakings of the kind is that a Government assessor or arbitrator, shall inspect the property in dispute and 'dispensing with the customary confusing mass of expert evidence, arrive at an award .on the figures submitted to him by the Parties, coupled with the results of his own Observations. The system might, with obvious advantages to the ratepayers and all 'Concerned, be applied to municipal improve- ments. It is a reform which is long over-due.

* * * The suffragettes—usually so keen on per- secuting Cabinet Ministers—gave Mr.Birrell's meeting the go-by. The arrangements for the Parr Hall meeting were so perfect that it livould have been far easier for a camel to Pass through the eye of a needle than for a 'female to have penetrated within the well- guarded precincts of the building. No attempt was made to way-lay the Chief Secretary for Ireland who, on taking his departure by the 9.50 train to Liverpool, was escorted to the Central Station by the Chief Constable (Mr. M. Nicholls) and a number of Plain-clothes detectives. Warrington has a record for peaceable political meetings which many another town might well envy. Saturday's demonstration was a triumphant success in every respect and for its admirable organization much credit is due to Mr. E. A. Sanders. *4*

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell does not always take an extreme point of view, even in the turton wood Parish Magazine, which has a reputation to live up to. His remarks in the Current issue take the form of a wellzeasoned reply to a local minister who has zecently animadverted on the present day Craze for pleasure. Mr. Mitchell contends that the craze does not necessarily //lean that the present generation is less godly than any of its forerunners, and he ,Proceeds to argue that the drab conditions of life and the hideousness of environments are responsible. The Church he adds should lay the blame at home and endeavour to improve the home life of the masses before blaming them for seeking relief in forms of pleasure Which are not the highest and which do not ..elevate the spirit as all true pleasure should. I.q r Mitchell has hit the nail on the head and Indicated one direction where Christianity fails through not being practical.

Presentation to Mr and Mrs Harry As ton, The great popularity of Mr Harry Ashton, secretary of the Warrington Football Club, in local sporting circles found tangible expression on Tuesday evening when, in the committee pavilion on the Wilderspool ground Mrs Ashton and he were presented with a handsome canteen of cutlery and a silver tea service, in celebration of their recent mariage. The gifts were some acknowledgement of the long and faithful services which Mr Ashton has rendered to the club for about thirty years in a variety of ways. Mr Ashton succeeded Mr E Warren as secretary to the club several years ago, and be has taken the utmost interest in its fortunes. He followed the Rugby Union - code as a player for 15 seasons, and was .for five years the captain of the team, in which he appeared as a forward. He assisted in the formation of the West Lancashire team for two years in the engage- ments with Cumberland He was also prominently associated with the formation of the Not them n Union, of which he was president six years ago, and for several years served on the ex-cutive Committee as representing Warri nptori. The dramatic possibilities of the situation are worked up to the highest pitch and the climax comes when the sash, the emblem of honour is torn from her, only to be recovered arid treasured by Yvonne. Miss Blackburn possesses a sweet and flexible voice and her interpretation of the songs "The, Lee Shore " and " Ever and ever mine" are most expressive. The dainty Yvonne is charmingly portrayed by Miss Carrie Hastings, who maintains the reputa- tion gained in leading parts in previous enterpri,es. It is as a singer that she excels, her methods being truly artistic, and her rendering of " Before the altar" is, as a piece of soulful and moving vocalization, the feature of the evening. As far as her acting is con- cerned, there is occasionally a disposition to treat the part too lightly and inconsequentially, undully hurry- ing some of her lines, instead of giving full utviard expression to the emotions, but in every other respect her work is excellent.

The voices of Miss Blackburn and Miss Hastings blend well in the duets "Little bird on weary wing " and "For lack of gold," which are given with charm- ing simplicity and feeling. One of the most con- spicuous successes of the opera falls to a new-comer in the person of Miss Ethel R Green, who, as the pert, irrepressible Choppinette, is the embodiment of vivaciousness. Her voice is not sufficiently matured to accord completely with the part of an old smuggler's .wife, but notwithstanding this limitation, she rises to the occasion with remarkable facility. Her natural freedom of gesture and action, together with smart- ness of verbal retort, make her impersonation most entertaining. Her quaint song and dance, "Just look at my sabots," are very ranartly performed and constitute one of the chief " hits" of the evening, a repetition invariably being demanded. A large share of the laughter-making must be credited to Mr Arthur Davies, as Bouillabaisse, the rough but inventive old smuggler, and Mr George Harding, as Petit Pierre, his very apt pupil in wrong-doing. Mr Davies has, of course, figured prominently in other undertakings of the s,ciety, but Bouillabaisse is his best achieve-nent. Loud-voiced, garrulous, bold in encountering foes at sea but living in dread of his sharp-tongued wife when ashore, this disreputable sea-dog largely dominates the comic incidents of " Paul Jones" and in Mr Davies's hands, full use is made of every ounce of humour. He is also thoroughly at home in a nautical dance which proves highly popular. Mr Harding plays up well to his colleague, the vacant expression of the novice unaccustcmed to " old hand's " devices being 11) well simulated as easily to deceive the unwary. The pair are excruciatingly funny when, disguised as fearsome Indians and flourishing tomahawks, they go through a curious song and weird dance. Mr J Newns, who took up the part of Bicoquet. at comparatively short notice, presents a capital study of the worldly-wise ship's carpenter of St Maio, amusing everyone by the shill with which he angles for Paul's f rtune while at the same time endeavouring to wriggle out of his share of the compact. Mr Wallace Ray is manly,confident Kesti el,skipper of a Yankee privateer and Miss Amy Rowe plays Quava, Malaquena's maid, in creditable style. Florence and its Art. Continuing his lectures on "Florence and its Art," Mr J E Phythian spoke at some length on "The painters of the Gothic time" in the Co-operative Hall on Saturday evening Councillor Henshall presided and said that those lectures, held under the auspices of the Ogden Trustees, were proving very popular in the town, and they had evidence of that in the large attendance assembled in the hall that evening. (Hear, hear.) As a member of the Museum Committee, he could say that it was their aim to try and educate the people and stimulate the desire for the artistic in the hearts of Warringtonians. He had visited the art galleries ou the Continent and had been very much impressed by what he saw. Mr Phythian remarked at the outset that the whole of the members of the Warrington Town Council should be sent abroad so that they could see how much better things were done there than here. They should, if studying the artists of the past, draw comparisons with the present day for the guidance of their own life and time. Florence was not merely a show place where wonderful works of art were produced, but it was a commercial city. The lecturer dealt with his subject in a highly interest- ing manner, and numerous pictures depicting the beauties of this ancient city were shown including the tof Cimabue and Giotti his pupil. In conclusion, Mr Phythian referred to the great revival of historical tableaux in this country. Other cities and towns had their pageants and he was sure Alderman Bennett would get one up for Warrington. (Applause).

Funeral of the Late Mrs James Richardson. The funeral of Mrs James Richardson, who passed away at her residence, Palmyra House, Springfield- street, on the 12th inst. took place at the Cemetery at noon on Saturday. Mrs Richardson, who had borne a long illness with great fortitude, was held in high esteem by a wide circle for her many estimable qualities and her support of good works. She took an exceptionally keen interest in the Warrington Children's Summer Camp at Prestatyn and also in the Aged People's Annual Treat, and. her services to each institution were highly appreciated. She leaves sone eon and two daughters. Prior to the interment a service was held in the Holy Trinity Church, the Revs E C E Carleton and W Bracecamp officiating. Mr W B Randall, organist, played appropriate music. The principal m'iurners were Mr James Richardson (husband), Mr Stanley Richardson (son), Dr H Joseph (son-in-,aw), Mr C Bouchier (son-in-law), Mr Whitham (brother), Mr Burdett (brother-in-law), Mr James Richardson, jun, and Mr W Richardson, jun, (nephews), Mr W Burdett (nephew), Mr J C Robinson, Mr Sidney Wallhead, Mr T Cooper, Mr J Main waring, Aldermen Smethurst and Forshaw,Councillors Birtles and Jenkins Mr Bouchier, Mr W Hodgkincon, Mr W S Haddock, Mr Mawdsley, Dr Peacocke, Mr John Hankinson and Mr R Cuppage. Many handsome floral tributes were received. The funeral arrangements were carried out by W Hodgkinson, Ltd, Bridge-street.

An " Odd Lot "In the Labour Market. The plea that, in consequence of an accident whilst following his employment, be had become a nonde- script or' odd lot "in the labour market was advanced on behalf of James McCaul, drayman, 11, Arpley- Walk, who brought a claini under the Workmen's Compensation Act, against Messrs Greenall, Whitley and Co, Ltd. Mr W B Forshaw appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr Oppenheim, of St Helens, represented the defendants. There was a pronounced conflict of medical opinion in the case, and his Honour Judge Stanger, K.C.,called in Dr Bowden as medical refereee. For the applicant it was stated that on October 19th, 1911, he was loading barrels on to a dray when his left hand was trapped between two barrels and terribly crushed. After having been under the care of Dr McKee he returned to work for ten days, but was dismissed on December 17th. Compensation was paid by the insurance company until Ap il ISth last. About ten days later they offered applicant 3:5 in full satisfaction of his claim, but he rejected it. Applicant stated that he had tried to obtain employment at a large number of places but as soon as his incapacity became known he was sent away. The medical evidence on his behalf was to the effect that the hand was permanently disabled.—The respondents sub- mitted that the applicant was quite capable of doing his work. Whilst he %Nl's working for ten days after the accident, he performed his duties to the entire satisfaction of the firm. The reason fa- his dismissal was incivility.—Doctors called by the firm stated that applicant's hand was not permanently disabled, and that by exercise it could be brought back to its normal condition. Dr Reynolds, of Mtnchester, suggested that the man was in a nervous state through brooding over the accident. —His Honour, intimating that the advice given to him by the medical referee was substantially in accord with the case presented by the respondents, awarded the applicant £5. As that amount had already been offered to the applict,nt by the insurance company, he would not grant costs.

"PAUL JONES."

GREAT SUCCESS FOR THE CROSFIELB OPERATIC SMELT.

Looking back over the notable series of enter- prises promoted by the Crosfiehl Amateur Operatic Society, we have no hesitation in placing the present week's performances of" Paul Jones" at the Royal Court Theatre first from the standpoint of artistic success. There are features of the production which undoubtedly mark it as superior to anything pre- viously attempted by the society. Principals and ' chorus alike have a thorough grip of the work in hand and, generally speaking, abandon themselves to the spirit of the opera with a lighteeme whole- heartedness quite exceptional in amateur companies. The result is that the stilted self-consciousness which is the bane of so many ventures of the kind is almost non-existent ; the performance never shows the slightest tendency lip drag, but is fresh and bright from beginning to end, delighting the eye and the ear by its succession of beautiful stage pictures end haunting music. The advance is especially noticeable in the singing of the chorus. A capital body of voices has been got together and the best possible use is made of them, the whole of the chorus work being characterised by refreshing crispness and attention to details of light and shade. The performances also reveal the pleasant fact that the society has some promising new talent to draw upon for, while most of the principals have gained success in previous operas, several per- formers make their tiebuts, and in each case the choice has been fully justified. The result is the more gratifying when one recalls the limited field to which the society has to look for its forces. All the vocalists are employee of the firm and only in the constitution of the orchestra has the outside profes- sional element been introduced. Without in any degree wishing to disparage what has been voluntarily done in this direction in the past, we think the pro- moters are to be commended on their boldness in making the new departure. A. carefully selected and perfectly drilled orchestra is of snch paramount importance that it is not worth while to leave anything to chance, and the instrumental work on this occasion is beyond reproach. Altogether the production affords the society every reason, for self-congratulation and, given a continuance of enthusiastic endeavour and loyal support, should make the future tolerably safe. From small beginnings the organization has gradu- ally expanded its work until this year the manage- ment decided upon the experiment of taking the Royal Court Theatre for a week. As far as stage accommo- dation is concerned, the advantage of the migration from the Parr Hall is too obvious to need empeasie, the scenic effects and the grouping of the comeaey being much more effective than has hitherto been found possible. We hone public patronage w ill prove generous enough to crown the week's run with success from the financial point of view. As in previous years, local charities are to benefit from the society's efforts —a fact which of itself should ho sufficient to com- mand appreciation. Most people have heard of Paul Jones, of buccaneer- ing fame. Whatever ceeflict there may be as to the true character of that nautical worthy, Planquette's opera certainly presents him in a most estimable light —in fact, compared with the personages who scorn him as an outcast, he almost merits a halo. The opera may possibly be dubbed " old fashioned " by devotees'of the modern musical play, but that matters little. The music—while generally straightforward end free from intricacies and eccentricities—is not by any means commonplace. Sparkling and tuneful, it is not of the thin, evanescent type which creates no lasting impression, nut calls for and repays serious and painstaking effort. There are some fine solce for the female voices and if, apart from the very effective choruses, concerted numbers are few, they are exceptionally pleasing. To many it may seem a grave initial drawback that the hero's part in the opera has—not without precedent—been allotted to a lady, but this does not detract from the realism of the piece to anything like the extent that may be imagined—indeed the female Paul Jones in this instance enacts her shate of the love passages with greater fervour and enthusiasm than one usually expects from the traditional amateur operatic lover. The story is full of incident, and there is much sc ipe for the realization of highly dramatic climaxes. The settings are picturesquely contrasted, and the chorus have in turn to fill the unaccustomed roles of fisher folk, society ornaments and naval men.

Although, of course, the merits of some of the principals are outstanding, all creditably contribute their quota to the generdl succeqs of the opera. First honours, by general consent, go to Miss Mamie Blackburn who, as Paul Jones, achieves quite a triumph. She is admirably fitted, by voice and hear- ing, for this attractive plirt ad infuses such a degree of dash and spirit into her acting as to make the

Warrington and District Cricket League

The annual- meeting of the Warrington and District Cricket Lague was held at the Roan Cow Inn, Bridge Foot, on Wednesday evening, Mr J W Travis presid- ing, A summary of the financial position of the League was submitted by the Secretary (Mr C E Hawthorn) wl:o stated that when the list of donations was completed, the League would have a small balance in hand. This was regarded as satisfactory in view of the exceptional expenditure incurred last season The Walker a Brewery Club intimated their intention of withdrawing from the League for the coming season and their decision was accepted with regret. Art application for admittance from Widnes St. Mary's C.C. was unanimously granted. The League have still other vacancies and clubs in the district are invited to join. The following officers were re-elected :—President, Colonel J Reid Walker, J.P. ; Vice-presidents, Sir Gilbert Greenall Bart., Harold Smith M.P., the Mayor (Councillor Dr Joseph), Mr W P Rylands, Mr J Fulton Smith, Mr R R Hutton, Mr J F Foster, Mr T Halton, Mr F Milling, Mr J A Rush- woi th, Councillors Dr McKee and R Hensha,ll ; Chair- man, Mr J W Travis; Hon. Secretary, Mr C E Haw- thorn ; Hon. Treasurer, Mr Hackney; Assistant Secretary, Mr H Daintith. In proposing the re-elec- tion of Mr Travis as chairman of the League, a tribute was paid to the business-like manner in which he had discharged his duties. Colonel. J Reid Walker was thanked for his kindness in acting as president, as were also the vice-presidents and the officers of the League.

GROCERS' DINNER.

THE MAYOR AND COUNCIL SCENES.

Council at its Worst in Public.

The grocers of Warrington have cultivated the fraternal spirit with great success in their organisaton - the Warrington and District Grocers' and Provision Dealers' ..kssociation—and the twelfth annual dinner, which was held at the King's Cafe on Thursday even- ing, was marked by a spirit of good fellowship which, for: the time being. banished all thoughts of trade rivalries. The function was a complete success from the social standpoint, Lr the programme was not over- burdened with speeches and there were many delight- ful musical items. About eighty members and friends assembled under the chairmanship of Mr W H Owen, the esteemed president of the association, who was supported by the Mayor (Councillor Dr Joseph), Messrs E Brown, W Monks, J W Kay, H Reading (vice-presidents),J Jackson (treasurer), T S Steel (solicitor), T Proud (secretary). The guests included Messrs H Grosvenor, W A Proud, W Flood, F Fieldhouso and a largo number of commercial representatives trout neighbouring towns. It is worthy of note that Messrs J Walirisle-y and H F Lenton have attended every dinner from the inaugura- tion of these popular functions. General regret was expressed at the absence of Councillor Wilkinson, an esteemed friend of the association. After dinner, excellently served by the Cafe Company, the usual toast list was gone through. The loyal toasts were submitted front the chair.

TRIBUTES TO WARRINGTON TRADESMEN.

The toast of "The Visitors " was proposed by Mr J W KAY, end responded to by Messrs H WALMSLEY, F LENTON, H GROSVENOR and Councillor J URMSON Mr WALAISLEY said the grocers of ‘Varringcon would stand ccmparison with any set of men in the country for honesty, uprightness and integrity. They bad no need to e ashamed of their trade, and their trade had no need to be ashamed of them. (Applause). Mr LENTON, contrasting his experiences ot the trades- men of Warrington with those of other towns, said that in Warrington he was alw,ys received with a warmth of feeling which made one feel at home whereas at Preston, for instance, the atmosphere was somewhat chilling and depressing. The population of Warrington could rest as,ured that they Aere in the very best hands when they placed their custom with the local grocers._ _

Cotincillor UIZAISON (Wallasey), said it was over fifty yeqrs since he came to Warrington as a boy and one of his first acquaintances was a grocer who carried on business in the neighbourhood of the Latchford level crossings. He began his own business career in Bridge-street and he remembered such old Warrington worthies as Sammy Blacking, Billy Cliff, "Pudgy " Clegg, " Bob 'Aw " (Robert Hall) and Tatney Dutton. Mr GROSVENOR said the grocers would bear com- parison with any body of tradesmen or professional men in this country. He himself was a tradesman, for he stood behind a counter a good many hours a day. (Laughter.) The tradernen of Warrington were of exceedingly high character and very pleasant men to coal with. •

Inquiries must be short, concise, and plainly written, and the name and address of the writer given to show that the inquiry is genuine. The replies are by a member of the profession, and every effort is made to secure accuracy, but we cannot assume any responsibility for the opinion or advice given. Mark all envelopes containing queries " Legal," and address them to the EDITOR," EXAMINER,' WARRINGTON. If you do not wish initials to be used in the reply please suggest a mom-de-plume.

P.W.—lf a Society believes that an insured person ought to take proceedings under the Workmen's Compensation Act, and the insured person refuses, then the Society may either (1) itself sue the employer, or (2) refuse to pay benefits to the insured. But if a Society chooses it may pay the benefits. Some Societies have special receipt forms in cases of this kind according in which the insured pledges himself to repay to the Society the money it has advanced in case compensation is obtained from the employer. _ . . _

All Societies do uot insist in every case on proof of marriage before paying the maternity benefit. But most Societies do, and it seems probable that in future the requirements of the Societies will be more stringent in this respect. The insured will be entitled to the full benefits of the Act, as though he lead never come under the special provisions of Section 47. _ _ _

RISH.—No. The landlord would only sell subject to the terms of the tenancy and the tenant simply exchanges one landlord for another. MENlo.—Copies of all wills proved in the principal registry or district registries since 1858 may be obtained by application to the Record Keeper, Principal Probate Registry, Somerset House, London.

J. T.—lf the borrower refuses to pay extortionate interest, and the money lender thereupon sues him, the Court has power to reduce the rate of interest if the transaction appears harsh and unconscionable. Tithe rate of interest is under all the circumstances excessive this is enough to cause the Court to regard the transaction as harsh, and to reduce the rate. But the Court will take into account the risks run by the lender in deciding the question of excessive rates of interest.
