MR. BIRRELL AT WARRINGTON.

GREAT SPEECH ON HOME RULE, FREE TRADE AND THE THE LAND QUESTION.

A Challenge to North=East Ulster.

===== 0002601_19130222_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
SUPPORT FOR MR. LLOYD GEORGE'S LAND POLICY.

"What is wanted in this country is Free Trade for the accumulation of wealth and wise laws for its distribution." In this terse, arresting sentence Mr Augustine Birrell, K.C., M.P., Chief Secre- tary for Ireland, in a great speech at War- rington on Saturday evening crystallised the economic watchwords of Liberalism. ' Although the major part of his oration, as was to be expected from the occupant of his important and difficult office, was devoted to: the Home Rule Bill, Mr Birrell could not resist the opportunity of dealing, in his inimitably genial and satirical sty le,with the present unhappy confusion of the Tory party on the subject of food taxes. He showed that, while this most obnoxious part of the Protectionist programme had osten- sibly been shelved, it was, like the discarded musket in the attic, ready for oduction whenever the electors were blind enough to entrust Mr Bonar Law and a Pro- tectionist Cabinet with the power of taxing the means of life. Then it was that Mr. Birrell stated the case for Free Trade in its true perspective—not as the be all and end all of our economic system,but as the only sure foundation on which the structure of equal opportunity and genuine social reform can be built. A more equitable distribution of the ' national wealth there must assuredly be—as Mr. Birrell pointed out, it is meaningless to talk to the starving man about Board of Trade statistics,—but by abandoning Free Trade we should be " killing the goose that lays the golden eggs." Mr. Birrell pointed to one way in which the lot of the masses can be immeasurably improved by expres- sing his full sympathy with Mr. Lloyd George's land campaign. It is evident that the scope of this most far-reaching of reforms will be much wider than many people have imagined for, besides wages and housing, full attention is to be paid to the scientific increase of agricultural production in the home land as a necessary precaution against the possible failure of food imports from the American Continent. On his favourite topic of Home Rule, Mr. Birrell was persuasive, logical and confident. In contrast to the tirades of Ulster leaders, there was not the faintest suggestion of rancour or bitterness in his remarks—and he even went so far as to offer the olive branch to the Protestant malcontents of the north east, and to ask them to formulate some definite demand in black and white before the Home Rule Bill again goes up to the House of Lords. Home Rule is bound to come, and Mr. Birrell is so con- vinced that a return to repression and coercion is impossible that he predicted that, even if the present Liberal effort were to fail, ' Mr Bonar Law in his first King's Speech would have to promise a measure of Irish devolution. "If you get rid of us " he declared "you don't get rid of the Irish problem." One could not fail to be impressed with the generous tone which characterised Mr. Birrell's references to Sir Edward Carson. He gave the King of the Ulsterites credit 'O7 llonesty of motive and love of his country. " I am also sure" added Mr. Birrell " that he is not a bigot, although sad necessity has compelled him of late to keep very bad company." In relentlessly search- ing style, Mr. Birrell examined the case for Ulster (or a portion of that province) and pressed the rebelliously inclined to declare exactly what they wanted. Did they wish to become a county of either England or Scotland, or to establish a Government of their own ?—the only alternatives to accept- ing an Irish Parliament. Saturday was the second occasion within a short period that Warrington has had the honour of welcoming Mr. Birrell. In December 1908 the Chief Secretary addressed a meeting at the Parr Hall when education and licensing were the burning topics of the moment and he recalled then—what he again delight- fully touched upon on Saturday—that his eat-tier memories of Warrington were associated with a meet- ing addressed by Mr Gladstone in the old nnbli, hall. In those days Messrs Gladstone and Grenfell were contesting Scuth West Lancashire against Nlessra Lros 4 and iurier. Quietly yet bitingly ironical was Mr Birrell's comment on the calibre of the respective sets of candidates. "Cross and Turner!" he repeated. " Well, well ! "—and nothing more. And he went on to remind the audience that Mr Gladstone's name still shines radiantly. As to the meeting itself, it was one of the most impressive political demonstrations ever held in the borough. Although the attendance was confined to men only, the building was crowded and enthusiasm reigned from beginning to end of the proceedings. The hall was brightly decorated, Union Jacks and the Irish national colour being the chief features of the scheme. Long before the time fixed for the opening of the meeting, the hall was filling rapidly, and the tedium of waiting was relieved by the singing of political songs. led by Mr Nathan Cook, with Mr H Payton, Mus. Bac. at the organ. A great burst of cheering heralded the apperance on the platform of Mr Birrell, Mr A FL Crosfield and the leaders of local Liberalistn. Mr C E Parker (president of the Liberal Association) was in the chair and among others on the platform were: Alderman Burton, Councillers Peter Peacock (chairman of the executive committee), John Wilkin- son (deputy-chairman) H Roberts, J Shaw, J Hawthorn, E Osborne,J T Lccker (secretary), Captain H G Roberts, Revs 'l' T James, W Cooper, S Johnson Widnes), and R W B Whiteway (Lcmm), Messrs J B Martindale (candidate for Widnes Division),T Locker, B Aitken, W Clegg, P O'Brien, G Walker, 0 Toole, W Bibby, Jos Wilkinson, J Jarman, M howarth, T Dale, J Longland, E Lloyd Jones, W H Sher burn, P Delaney, J Critchley, C Jackson Holmes, J H Ogden (agent for Northwich Division), A Mounfield, P Carter, and W Russell. _ - -

Apologies for non-attendance were received from a number of Lancashire and Cheshire M.P.'s, also from Aldermen Tinnion and James Evans, Mr P M Oliver, Mr J A Ransom° and others. _

Mr Ziba Armitage, than whom there is no more earnest and censistent Liberal in the Warrington district, after referring to his absence as "one of the disappointments which advancing years and precarious health bring in their train," expressed his best wishes for a good and enthusiastic meeting. "We Liberals" he wrote "are not greatly disturbed by the terrible distress of the Tory party. A Liberal policy which pleased them would be a poor affair and a Liberal Ministry which trimmed its measures to catch their support would very quickly lose ours." (Applause).

legislation. Coming to the National Insurance Act, Mr Parker quoted a poem by William Watson entitled " True Impet ialism." After referring to the lust for conquest and to the attention that was being diverted to our " far flunx battle line," the poet spoke of the foes and the needs which remained to be dealt with at home. He concluded

Your savage deserts howling near Your wastes of ignorance, vice and shame, Is there no room for victories here, No field for deeds of fame? Arise and conquer while you can The foe that in your midst remains And built within the mind of man The Empire that abides.

The recognition of the thought contained in those words induced Mr Lloyd George —(cheers)—and the Government to pass the great National Insurance Act which they believed would prove of more value to the countay than many Dreadnought. (Cheers.) It was an instrument designed for the safeguarding of life instead of its destruction. The session which had just c3ncluded would be memorable for the passing through the HOUSE 3 of Commons of the great Act which had been so long and earnestly desired by the Irish people. (Cheers.) "We are pond of our Government " continued Mr. Parker, " We are proud of our Prime Minister—(cheers)—and we are proud of our Chief Secretary for Ireland (Loud cheers). I think Mr Birrell would be the first to acknowledge that the Liberal party all over the country has given the Government a consistent back- ing. (Cheers). Although in Warrington we have not been able to do as much as we would, by sending to Parliament, while these great Bills were going through, one who would vote on our side, still the Liberal party in Warrington is staunch and true and to-night we cordially welcome Mr Birrell to our town." (Loud cheers).

when they served their country, there would be no dearth of men in this country, because old England was pre-eminent in the readiness of her citizens to give voluntary service where they could. (Cheers). He for one believed that we were rapidly approaching the time when it would not be regard,ed as merely selfish and extremely short-sighted to work people to the point of exhaustion and rob them of opportunities for leisure, but as the very negation of patriotism to adapt that discredited and out-of-date policy. On the other side, some little stigma would then rest upon every man who, having the leisure and capacity, dii not in his turn, in one way or another—and we were not all going to be of one pattern—render some service voluntarily to his country. (Cheers).

===== 0002601_19130222_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
TRUE IMPE RALISA.

The CHAIRMAN passed under review the great landmarks in the political hi4tory of the last six years. MR CROSFIELD MOVES THE RESOLUTION.

Air A H CROSFIELD, who had a great reception, moved the following resolution :—" That this meetiog expresses its confidence in his Majesty's Govern- ment and appreciation of its measures for the well-being of the people. It congratulates the Government, and especially on this occasion, the Chief Secretary for Ireland, the Right Hon Augustine Birrell, on the pissing by the people's House of the great measure for the self-government of Ireland and pledges itself to adopt all constitutional means to support the Government in their resolution to make this and other measures law."

Mr Cr,sfield was in flue speaking form and devoted a thoughtful and convincing speech to some of the out- stanOing questions cf social reform which have been before the country of late. Referring to the euthuAastic tone of the meeting, he said he was glad he detected no note of any kind of discouragement because, for a little while, they had fallen out of the Liberal ranks, as far as ttie representation of Warrington was concerned. "If you do not feel discouragement" he continued "I assure you I don't and, what is more, I should not be lit co be sitting next to Mr Birrell—(cheers)--- if I were in any dejected frame of mind,b2cause,if there is one man, above all others, who in the House of Commons and throughout the country, has a reputation for che,-ry and convinced optimism it is our guest of this evening." (Applause.) Mr Crosfield went on to express admiration of the clear-sighted and far-sighted political statesmanship of their illustrious leader, Mr Asquith. (Cneers.) They were proud of the intellec- tual mastery and the dignity, which was nut seen in every quarter of politics nowadays—laughter)—and of the inspiring lead which the Prime Minister had given to the progressive forces. (Cheers.) They also joined withlthe Chairman in the respect they felt for the great hard working, and brilliant band of men who formed a Cabinet such as he verily believed, in point of all- round capacity and gifts, surpassed any Cabinet in the recc ids of English political history. (Cheers ) GIANTS AND PIGMIES.

Mr CROSFIELD next recalled a conversation he had had in London with one of the most experinced and s4gacious Labour members, who had given him much information and advice in connectipu with the work or the International Association for Labour Legisla- tion. Discussing the personnel of the two trout Benches, the Labour representative said: "On the Opposition side I look upon them as little better than political pigruies, but on the Government front bench you have a race of intellectual and political giants" (cheers). In a passing allusion to the fiscal contro- versy, Mr Crosfield said that, looking back over the last few generations, they could well liken Free Trade to a great fertilising stream which had tlJvved through this country, pouring itself into the great ocean of trade, reprosented by our home market and our vast over-seas conuuerce.• The idea of trying to darn up that stream and force it artificially into unnatural channels seemed to him preposterous for the reason that, with the stagnant water that would result, they would also get a great deal of the atmosphere of corruption and degradation of public life from which, 'in the main, we were happily so free in England. (Cheers). POLITICAL QUICK-CHANGE ARTISTES.

As to their opponents, continued Mr Crosfield, he thought, in the language of the Prime Minister, that they had added not a little to the gaiety of nationa by their kaleidoscopic political career and taeir per- formances as quick-chance artistes. (Laughter.) But as for a definite policy, they could see no trace of any at the present moment, unless it be the policy of political confusion. (War, hear.) Turning to the record of the Government, Mr Crosfield said their great constructive edifice of reform was not being reared haphazard; it had been thought out and planned with wisdom and fore-sight, and stops had been taken to pay their way beforehand, by means of the great people's Budget. (Cheers.) With regard to old age pensions,they were happy to recall that it was their guest of that evening who first said that in the history of Englaud, there was no measure of social reform which had shed more sunshine into humble homes than the Old Age Pensions Act. (Cheers.) Then there was the great National Insurance Act. (Caeers.) If it were only for the extent to which it had given shield, breastplate and buckler to so many millions of our people,to enable them to ward off "the slings and arrows of outrageous fortune," end to provide against the cruelty of circum- stance and unmerited hardships, that Act would be memorable and glorious for all time. (Cheers.) Ile had heard a cool and skilled public official, who could not by any stretch of the imagination be described as a partizan, declare: " The more I look into that Act, the more I am struck with the fclly of those members of the Conservative party who look upon Mr Lloyd George as a mere gas-bag, and fail to realize the qualities of real statesmanship that are written in every page of that measure." (Cheers.) But when one con- sidered the direct effect which the Act was bound to have upon housing and health questions, by making a reality of many other measures which had unfortunately been allowed to become dead letters, one saw how far it would help to carry us along the road of healthy and progressive life. It would no longer pay the individual or Corporation to own slum property or an employer of labour to encourage conditions of work in his factory or workshop that would develop sickness beyond the normal point. In connection with the slum problem, however, it must be borne in mind that they could not demolish houses without providing suitable dwellings in their place. Overcrowding of the workers could not be tolerated. Touching upon the question more particu- larly as it concerned agricultural workers, Mr Crosfield said that while they recognized that it would be unnecessary,and even an impertinence to interfere with powerful trade unions, which were capable of looking after themselves—(cheers)—there were many other workers who were not strong enough to defend their own interests. That raised the great question of the minimum wage which had already been brought forward with reference to the agricultural districts and which must ultimately become a practical issue, as far as the town worker was con- cerned. To complete the picture, they would have the question of excessive hours of labour dealt with too, so that men might have a chance of leading the civilized life of the modern citizen. The outcome would be seen in the ability of men to put in vigorous, alert work for a reasonable number of hours, and return home refreshed, instead of having had every ounce of energy extracted from them, with the prospect of leisure for self-culture and also for public activity in connection with such subjects as education, sanitation and local politics. ENCOURAGING THE TERRITORIALS.

The Territorial Force, continued Mr Crosfield, would also benefit largely from the improved conditions.lf only we gave men adequate leisure and ceased to handicap them—as they had been very unfairly handicapped—

(Cheers.) "They say " concluded Mr Crostleld "that the Irish Secretaryship has been the grave of many a statesman, but that will never be said of our guest of this evening who. I verily believe, is going to share with the late Sir Henry Campbell Bannerman and Mr Asquith, in one of the proudest triumphs of modern times." (Loud cheers.)

TORIES WOULD HAVE TO GR ANT HOME RULE. Supposing our friend Mr Boner Law were to obtaic at the next election that small majority for which, honest man thit he is, he is always ready to pay a full marketable price—(laughter)—and he were to come back to Parliament and sit upon the front bench, be would have to concoct the King's speech. The first duty of a Prime Minister when he meets a new Parlia- ment is to compose a speech ahich is read by the Kitlgf and which represents the policy of the new Ministry. Mr &mar Law, I am sure, would call to his assistance that R arne body of able men who helped him to prepare Lis Ashton speech. (Laughter and cheers.) We have heen told that that remarkable utterance has since been suppressed as unfit for family reading. (Laughter.) That marvellous speech was, as you knono project of his own unaided brain; it was a limited company affair ; it was passed by a board of directors, after full consideration. (Renewed laughter.) Well, these'same gentlemen will now help him in the concoction of his new King's speech, and I am as certain as I am standing here it will contain a paragraph somewhat to this effect: " And, with regard to Ireland, his Majesty's Ministers propose to lay on the table of the House at a conveni- ent moment a measure which, whilst representing unimpaired the authority of the Imperial Parliament, and in no way affecting the integrity of the Empire, will yet devolve upon the Irish people some greater mfasure of control over their own affairs." In other words, BY GETTING RID OF US YOU DON'T GET RID OF ROME RULE. (Cheers). I am certain of this, that after the experi- e,ces we have had during the past six er seven years and after the pasFage of this Bill, no Parliament ever likely to assemble again at Westminster Rill authorise any Govern- ment to suppress the passionate desire of the Irish people to govern themselves (Cheers.) There are some things that cannot be done, atti that is one of them. (Hear, near.) See what it involves. You say to the Irish people : "Give up self-government, give up the dream, the hope, the aspiration ; we won't have it, we shall pay no attention to it. Tear it out of your hearts. Pull down O'Connell's statute in Dublin, and put up one to Sir Edward Carson in its place. (Laugh- ter.) Obliterate all the memories of hundreds of years; forget all that you learnt from your fathers, and for- get all that you have been teaching to your children ; abandon all these things as idle hopes, wicked dreams entire delusions. Come and be governed by us !" THE CANDIDATE FOR WIDNES.

Mr J B MARTINDALE, prospective candidate for Widnes, seconded the resolution in a racy speech which greatly appealed to the audience, He said that when he (the Breaker) was a babe in swaddling clothes, Mr Birrell had already contested the Widnes Division. One thing he (Mr A,lartindale) had discovered since he became associated with that constituency, was that the people had not forgotten the days when Mr Birrell put, up that gallant fight. It could to said of many Govern- men's that they had been lacking in imagination, and particularly with regard to Ireland's aspirations. Liberalism was stronger to-day than it was twelve months ago, but what about their opponents? " Satan finds some mischief still for idle hands to do" (Laughter). That saying had been made a living reality during the past year. (Hear, hear). Mr Boner Law, mistaking a rocket in the East, decided that the sun had risen. The rocket went up as the result of a Tory victory in a three-cornered contest. The sun rose and what did it show ?—a fog in the Tory ranks very much worse than the fog we had last week. (Laughter.) When the Liberal party appealed to the country it would be on a definite policy. When they hoisted their flag, it would not be necessary a day or two afterwards to haul it down to half-mast, and to nail in its place a letter of explanation. (Laughter). The resolution was then put to the meeting and carried, with one dissentient.

MR. BIRRELL'S SPEECH.

Striking Tribute to Mr. Crosf leld.

Mr BIRRELL had a most enthusiastic reception on rising to adure,s the meeting. His speech was couched in that vein which makes him a favourite with all classes of audiences. It sparkled with witticisms, yet underlying it all was strength of conviction, po%er of argument and remarkable lucidity. He said :—This is the first day of my holiday—(laughter)—and this is how lam spending it, unlike all those other runaway members of Parliament who very wisely are elsewhere. I am delighted to find myself Once again in Warrington, and addressing a Warrington audience. lam glad for various reasons. First of all because lam a Lancashire man born and bred, secondly becau.e I am always glad anywhere to do a stroke for the Liberal cause, and, thirdly, lam very glad—l am doubly and trebly gh.d— because

MEMORIES OF GLADSTONE.

Ile maintained that proposition on a score of Lanca- shire platforms. Ile was assailed with an abuse which is almost, despite the efforts of Mr Boner Law, out of date. (Laughter.) He was called a renegade, a torn- cont, a blasphemer, aad a robber of God. The Excited population of North-East Ulster declared that were any such infamous proposal carried into law they would throw the Crown into the Boyne, they would set up for themselves, and they would not survive the degrada- dation and the disgrace of seeing the Episcopal Church in Ireland disestablished. Is was done all the same. (laughter)—and now you would not find even among the bishops, and the deans, and the dignitaries, and toe incumbents, and the curates of that Church, a single voice that would be raised in favour of the restoration of their former position , They now claim relatively a small part of the popu atiou among their adherents, but they enjoy throughout the length and breadth of that island a respect and a feeling of friendship and goodwill, which was never there whilst they usurped and assumed an authority and a connection to which they had no right. (Cheers.) Well, these things are a ramble. What vvus true of the Established Church is true now of the great cause with which during the last months we in the House of Commons have been so closely associated --namely, the passage through that House of a Home Rule Bill for Ireland. (Cheers.) We are going on with it ; of that you may rest assured. (Hear, hear). Coming down here in the train I read a piece of information in the newspaper which gave me great regret, for it was that the vulture at the Zoological Garden in London which menOs ago hatched an egg, having most diligently sat upon it for fifty-eight days, last night put an end to its young life. It ate its own chick. (Laughter). THE LORDS AND HOME RULE. We don't propose to follow the example of that most unmaternal bird. We have been sitting for fifty-eight days upon our Home Rule Bill and we have hatched, and although it has undergone a rebuff in " another place," we, fortunately, beforehand made arrangements —(cheers —which will enable it in a few months to be sent up again and again, only what happens to it the third time will not matter. (Laughter.) Gentlemen, lEmust ask your patience for a few minutes while I address myself a little to that great subject. I feel in a certain sense bound to do so by Vii tue of the great responsibilities of the high office I hold in connection with that country, also by the unremitting toil of the last six years, and also, I am free to confess by the bent and inclination of my own mind, because I think this subject of self-government for Ireland is a great subject It is an educational subject ; it is a subject the more you think about the better qualified you will be to discharge in the future your duties as upholders and supporters of representative government and as deter- mined believers in the principles of self-government. (Cheers.) Well, gentlemen, some time ago, in this hall, the inhabitants of Warrington were addressed by a most distinguished man, whose absence front the Home Rule debates during the last month or two we all greatly deplor.. I refer to IR EDWARD CARSON,

who has been unable to be present owing to the serious illness of his wife. I myself am able fully to sympathise with anyone who, whilst he is engaged in a prat Parliamentary fight, has anxiety at home. lam sore we all deeply and profoundly sympathise with Sir Edward Carson. I freely admit he is a distinguished man and a very able man, and, I am sure, an honest inftil—(A VOICE: "No ")—and a lover of his count' y. I believe him to he all these things. lam also sure he is not a bigot, although sad necessity has compelled him of late to keep very bad company. He may at this moment be a hero in the eyes of Belfast, but it is not so very long ago—by no means long ago—that, in consequence of the very generous support he gave t ) me while I was passing the Universities Bill through the House of Commons, and the generous support he had also given earlier in the session, earlier in the lifetime of the Parliament, to an Act altering the Coronation oath, taking out of the mouth of a new King words of abuse attacking a central form of belief of the great majority of the Trish people—in conse- quence of that support of the University Bill and the alteration of the Coronation oath, Sir Edward Carson could not have shown his face in Belfast. But now for the moment he espoused their cause and he is recog- nised as their leader ; he in no way represents the bitter feeling of a small fraction of the population who may think he does ; but he does not However, he came down here and addressed a meeing in this hall, and he presented with much vehemenle his case against Home Rule.

NO ALTERNATIVE.

I meet that case by a simple statement of absolute belief, and that belief is this, that whatever you are going to do in Ireland, and whatever you try and mean to do in Ireland, be you Unionist or anything else, you can no longer govern her except by conceding to her this great gift of self govertment,—(cheers)—which all the representatives from every part of Ireland except a small corner in the north-east have for thirty years per- sistently demanded. DELUSION FOR PLATFORM CONSUMPTION has been invented, and that is that the Irish people do not really want Home Rule at all. We are getting rather into deep waters upon these subjects. The Welsh People do not want disestablishment, and the Irish people do not want Home Rule. (Laughter.) You say " Why in the name of commonsense do they return, year after year, election after election, men who have the effrontery to say, both to the electors end to the House of Commons, when they get there, " The one thing we do want is Home Rule.'" It seems to me to cut the whole basis from under the ground of representative government. (Hear, hear.) If the con- stituents say to a member "Go to Parliament and say so and so" and he goes into Parliament and says SO and so, ,A hat is the use of anyone saying, " His con- stituents in Warrington did not know whet he was talk- ing about. He simply said it because his wife or mother-in-law told him, but at the bottom of his heart he does not care about it at all." (Laughter) It is a sheer and downright delusi..n. The people of Ireland are less violent than they were, because they have m re and more confidence in toe democracy of this country. They are more prosperous than they were, but their Prosperity in no way interferes with their patriotism It is all very well for Lord Lansdowne to say : " If you go to a poor, small farmer in Ireland and say You aro paying a purchase annuity at £l5 a year for 40 or ,50 years, at the end of that time you will be a free- nclder. Suppose, instead of paying £l5 a year, you PM' £lO. All I ask you to do in exchange is to give up your INcionalist "

LORD LANSDOWNE'S SNEER.

And then Lord Lansdowne turns to his audience in the House of Lords and says : " Have you any doubt what this man will do ? " Well, of course, their lord- Ships They signified their approval, but then 37.011 see they are a house of landlords ; there was not a Single tenant sitting there—(laughter)—so the sneer was an easy one. Anyone can sneer in that kind of audience, but supposing by some chance someone had come in from outside arid to the Irish landlords who were there lii large numbers with pockets full of money obtained 'rim the sale of their land, suppose they had put the crucial test. Suppose I was to go to an Irish landlord Slid say, " Look here, you have got a goOd many acres still unsold. Suppose we can arrange to give you a bonus of six or ten millions More—you have already had twelve millions— SIX or ten millions more in order to enable you to sell the rest if your land, and all I ask in exchange is that You should give up your Unionist principles." If any- body bad said that they would have tb,ught it very rude. You may sneer at the absent, bat not at the Present, who are always supposed to be animated by the loftiest motives. (Laughter and cheers). It is a

POOR, MEAN, SHABBY ARG UMENT for anybody to attempt to express the hopes, feelings, nd abpirationr, political and racial, of a nation in terms of cash. It cannot be done. People are fond of Roney, and people will do a great deal for money, hut nations are not to be bought and sold. Nothing is Plainer in this long controversy than that it is coming to a close, and ihote of us in this room, without living to be very much older than we are, may rationally hope to see the day when it is at an end. (Cheers).

All I say in regard to that is this. THE GREATEST INTEREST.

UNCLE CANADA " Wasn'

Those who live in towns are sometimes apt to forget the enormous importance of agricultural interest, but it is still the greatest interest of the country. It, is of immense value We get our food supplies largely enough from Canada, from America, and from other places. But nothing is more remarkable, as all persons who deal in provisiors will tell you, than the shifting of these markets. A few years ago we got millions and inillicns more pounds of bacon from America than we are getting now. The secret is that AmericA is con- suming more of its own food. Canada, for many years to come, is a great granary. Its population, however, is filling up, and the time will come when Canada will require, perhaps not the whole of its great wheat- growing crops, but a greater proportion than now. It is, therefore, of supreme importance to this country, if we are to maintain the great population which %Ye at present do. that the fertility of 04 soil should he increased by every scientific method. There are those who tell us that science, if properly besought and instructed, can, tell us much whereby the fertility of

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

WORKHOUSE QUESTION.

The trouble respecting the entertaining of friends by officials of the Warrington Union recalls an experi- ence of my own in aeother part of the country many years ago. The Master f the heal Workhouse was left a widower with two children. After sounding certain of the Guardians, privately, he re-married on the assumption that his wife would be appointed matron But it " didn't come (if" that is, the wedding did, but the Board referred an txperienced matron and chose one accordingly. Then there were lively doings. The Master, who said he had been misled by the members whom be had consulted, had to submit to a substantial reduction of salary on account of the maintenance of his wife and one of the children who went out to NVOI k but continued to live in the house ; and there was constant disagreement with the matron, who knew her work and would brook no interference. There was no peace however, until both got situations elsewhere. .4:,1 A WEEK.

According to a summary in the last issue of this paper, the Rev H Ogden has been preaching at Golborne-street on ";.CI a week and the Gospel." It reminded me of the case of a Congregational minister in the very place of Mr Ogden's nativity whose stipend for well nigh a score years only amounted to LI a week. I hear that lately the operations of the central fund of the denomination have for the most part put an end to such scandals. The Baptists, in whose ministerial ranks there are similar hard cases, are just now wrestling with the like problem, and are meeting with a good deal of encouragement There is reason to hope that ere long the poorer of the regular preachers in both bodies will be assured of a minimum stipend of .100 or a little more. SPANISH PRISONER SWINDLE.

Of this, which was the subject of a note in last Saturday's "Examiner," I have known simething in every town in which I have worked during the last thirty years or more. I am acquainted with one manufacturer upn whom two attempts at imposition were made with an interval of twenty years between. On each occasion he was addressed as " Dear sirs and relatives," the Spanish writer pretending that he had married a member of the recipient's family, and she was dead, and that his chief anxiety was in tespect of their daughter, and he was very much more concerned for her safety than for his buried treasure, vast as the sum was, so that the Englishman was welcome to the bulk of it as long as he was willing to take charge of the girl.

MEETING THEIR MATCH. Once when a well-to-do man in the district in which I resided at the time was thus approached, we arranged to have a bit of amusement at the expebse of the unscrupulous Spaniards. My friend promply replied to the first communication and asked for further particulars. These were soon forth- coming, and he appeared to rise like a gudgeon to the bait. After more correspondence, he pretended to agree to a proposition as to preliminary expenses to be advanced by himself. He thereupon received what purported to be a legal document drawn by a notary public, binding the sender to pay to him at least one- fourth of the hidden (and ever elusive) fortune on the simple condition that he should advance ;£2.00 to enable that supposed political prisoners' friends to arrange for the recovery of the money. At this interesting juncture, of course, we dropped the correspondence. PEDESTRIANIST

Some years ago there was a craze fr walking, just. as more recently there has been one in relation to what was termed "standard bread." The generality are no longer enamoured of either, but individual enthusiasts remain. These, to say nothing of a Manchester club for the purpose, include the well- known Filey parson, who has pretty well stumped the continent, and—to come much nearei- home Mr. Hi ighouse "the tramping coroner." The latter advises pedestrians first of all to get a good shoe- maker to make them boots to fit. Once, when on this favourite topic, he said that; if the boots did not fit and then the man shot the shoemaker, and the case came under his jurisdiction, he would feel like letting the man cif on the ground of justifiable killing. He further advised those going in for walking to eat what they fahciad. As to drinking, he had never it. iven up the habit of having his pint of beer after a 20-mile walk.

BOOTS AND TEMPERAMENT. I well remember a writer in the " Atlantic Monthly "insisting in like manner that walking is fundamentally a matter of boots. This authority, without descending to minute details, said something like this : So the boot fit, the sole be adequate, and the heel not loftily inclined, choose your own wear, and you shall know content. There be some that favour tubber heels, and here again each man is his own arbiter; yet this, at least, is not to be forgotten or lightly overpa.sed ; there is a tang in the sharp crunch of a hard heel on fair roachmetil that greets not him who goes delicately on rubber. Let tempera- ment decide. IN PRAISE OF WALKING.

I am so desirous of a pedestrian revival that I readily yield to the temptation to draw a little more from that same source. Walking (concludes the latter contributor) is not merely moving two legs rhythmi • cally over certain intervals of ground. It is the

primal arid the only way to know the world, the orliberate entering into an inheritance, whose parts ate wind and weather, sky and prospect, men and animals, and all vital enjoyment The bicycle has some advantages in point f speed, but it is a foe to observation. All carriages, whether propelled by 11 rse or motor, destroy all feelings of achievement. The very word " mile " is a walker's word mille pissus - a thousand double-paces. So the Roman legions measure their conquering advances; so the legion of pedestrians estimates its conquest of the d,y. "So m•tuy thousand buffets have mine own two feet given the resisting soil 'twixt t.uir and sun ; so many thousand times have the g od muscles of calf and thigh lent their elastic f .rce." What has the dusty reader of figures on a dial to match with that? ALL THE DIFFERENCE.

Some limo ago a wag succeeded by a simple devict in playing a practical joke on a young eporter of my acquaintance. Running into the iflic. in a state of apparent excitement he told the eir):er news-gatherer that a labourer had just fallen off a ladder Mt. high Understanding that his informant had witnessed the accident he pressed for details, framing his questions is such a way that the joker, after declaring that the man had entirely escaped injury, wan constrained to admit that the fall was from the bottom rung of the 60ft. ladder ! LIKE UNTO IT.

It brought to my mind another ancient " wheeze " about a man "dropping a hundred feet into ecalding water" and being n..) worse for it. As the reader has already guessed, even if he does not happen previou-ly to have met with this friend of my youth, the explana- tion is that the feet which he dropped into boiling water were pig's feet. LENT.

_ It would be possible to enlarge considerably upon what I wrote as t.) this a week ago. It may surprise some of my readers to learn that well within living memory the statutes commanding fasting in Lent weie part of the law of the land. 1 was nine years old when they were repealed. I must hasten to add that the last attempt to enforce the observance was made by James 11. about a sear before he was swept off his throne. In 1687 he issued a proclamation enjoining the abstinence from meat (hiring the Lent, but saying that on certain conditions for giving alms to the poor, licenses to eat meat in any part of England might be obtained from an office in St Paul's Churchyard. PENALTIES FOR BREACH.

Going farther back in history we find that penalties for breach of the Lenten fast were enforced by some Protestants, and by no means exclusively by Roman Catholics. I read that in 154 S an Act of Parliament insisted on the Lenten fast by "imposing a penalty of ten shillings and ten da3s' imprisonment, for the first cffence, and twenty shillings and twenty days for its repetition." In the reign of Elizabeth the penalties were increased to sixty shillings and three months' imprisonment. The informer received half the fine under the Act of 1545. By the Act of Queen Elizabeth, the Crown, the informer, and the poor of the parish shared the fines in equal portions, so that there was some temptation for neighbours to act as spies on each other. There is abundant evidence that these regulations were unpopular, and no wonder. IN DULGENCES.

If only for the sake of the younger generaticn of readers, of whom comparatively few seem to know anything of the subject, it is well for once in a while to point out that we have progressed enormously during the last 100 years or so, and that the ancestors of these same careless young folk did and suffered much t win the liberty which they apparently esteem so lightly. True, it w as always possible, under certain conditions, to obtain a licence to indulge in flesh during Lent; but fancy being obliged to do that ! Prior to the Act of Elizabeth these dispensations were granted by the Council. Thus in the time of Edward VI a license was granted under the Privy Seal to one gentleman for himself and all who dined with him "to eat meat and dishes made of milk either in Lent or on any other fast days freely and without punishment," and the licence was to last his whole life. The Puritans set themselves to break down Lenten observance One writer of the period says : - "I have often ntniced that if any superfluous feasting or got maudis- ing, paunch-cramming assembly do meet, it is so ordered that it must be either in Lent, upon a Friday or a fasting, for the meat doth not relish well except it be sauced with disobedience and contempt of authority." By the bye, in 1635 the Corporation of Hull asked the Archbishop of York Lir a general dis- pensation for the inhabitants by reason of the prevalence r.f the Plague. The prelate replied that he had no power to grant a general dispensation, but such as were sick might obtain licences from their parish priests under a medical certificate. A WORD IN FAVOUR.

After all, there is s mething to be said in favour of periodical abstention from flesh meat. aud even of absolute fasting. Many medical authorities have of late 3 ears taken to strongly recommending at least partial fasting at stated times—purely in the interests of physical health, that is ; and then there is the drastic and Spartan remedy which is the subject of Upton Sinclair's latest book. However, for most of us I fear this must remain a counsel of perfection.

There has been a very insistent demand amongst those who have the training of the young for a bright and popular book which shall tell in an attractive manner the wonderful story of the Free Chtyches. On the suegestion of religious leaders the Rev J G Stevenson has undertaken the difficult task of presenting to the young mind the tales of courage and bravery which have secured for the Free Churches that measure of liberty and freedom which they now possess. This book is written with all Mr Stevenson's charm of style and while addressing the children in language they understand, he will thrill his youthful readers with a sense of joy and pride in the great deeds of their fore- fathers. This book is to be shortly issued by the National Free Chnrch Council under the title of "Fathers of our Faith."

THE LAND QUESTION.

_ Then there is my poor, dear friend,Mr Lloyd George. (Cheers ) He has got into great trouble with the Tory party. He is such a terrible fellow. Yet they called Gladstone a thief while he was living. I do not much care for those wen who " applaud the hollow ghost and blame the living man." We venerate the great name and memory of Gladstone and we derive inspiration from his example and enthusiasm from his words. But we have to go on working while it is yet day. The titre will come when we, too, will be shadows on the wall like him so far pt this world is concerned. (Cheers.) COUNCILLOR ROBERTS'S QUESTIONS FOR MR. HAROLD SMITH.

Councillor HENRY Houma's, in proposing a vote of thanks to Mr Burrell, recalled the fact that he too was present at the meeting which Mr Gladstone addressed in the old public hall. That was the occasion on which as a boy, he (Councillor Roberts) received his baptism of fire as a Liberal worker. He remained a member of the Liberal party to-day because, having hal a pretty considerable share in the administration of local affairs, he was satisfied that it was the putting into legislative form of Liberal opinions which was going to do more for the service of the helpless and the poor than any other form of legislation. (Cheers.) Something was said by Mr Birrell of the time when Mr Bonar Law was to be Prime Minister. He (Councillor Roberts) was not one of those who believed that they were going to see Mr Boner Law Prime Minister. There might have been the possibility some twelve months ago, if a successful snap division had been taken and the country, in the throes as it then was of misrepresentation, fraud and deceit with regard to the Insurance Bill, might have temporarily taken leave of its senses and sent Mr Bonar Law to power. But what a wondrous chang4 had oocurred in the mean- time He ha.r.: no expectation of seeing Toryism predominant in the next twenty years. (Cheers). What earthly reason was there?

STRANGE FISHES.

P e.'N".,/\,"...•,•"...‘,.•,‘N.el iCHILDREN'S CORNER, Conducted by UNCLE CALEB. NIINee",..WeNeisIANI°V4V

There is a fish in India which can climb Therefore it is called the climbing perch,— though, in point of fact, it is not a perch at all.

This singular creature has a little cistern of water in its head, so that the awful droughts which often afflict its native country have no terrors for it. When the little fre sh-water pool in which it lives dries up in the summer, as it often does for days or even weeks at a time, this queer fish stays affive by means of the water in its head. "Water on the brain," which kills human beings, keeps the climbing perch alive. "But," you ask, "why is it called a 'climb- ing perch ' ? " Because it can walk by means of its lower fins; awl it can also hitch along by means of its side -fins.

Sometimes when the home-pool dries up, the fish's burrow into the mud and lie there torpid uatil the rains refill the little lake.

Other.; seem to prefer to shuffle along overland until they find another pool. It used to be said that these fishes climbed cocoanut palm trees in quest of the milk. One reliable witness testified that he caught one on the stem of a tree five feet from the

ground. Another strange fish is the mud-skipper of Burmah and West Africa. It can stay in water only a little while at a time, or else it will drown. On the other hand, it cannot stay long on the land. It seems to be com- pelled to take a dip in the river once in every few minutes.

These fish have queer, telescopic eyes, which they poke out of their heads and turn about in a startling way. On the whole, they look more like tadpoles than fish. When they are tired they prop themselves up on their fins much as a man rests his elbows on a table.

THIS WEEK'S STORY. NO NEED FOR HURRY. A long-haired man walking along the street met a little boy, who asked him the time.

"Ten minutes to nine," said the man. "'Well," said the soy," at nine o'clock get your haircut." And he took to his heels and ran, the aggrieved one after him.

_ ... Turning the corner, the man ran into a policeman, nearly knocking him over. " What's up ? " said the policeman. The man, very much out of breath, said: "You see that young urchin running along there? He asked me the time, and I told him. Ten minutes to nine,' and he said, 'At nine o'clock get your hair cut." " Well," said the policeman slowly taking out his watch, "what are you running for? You've got eight minutes yet."

YES OR NO? (A Morning Tonic). " No " to the wrong, and "Yes" to the right: "No" to what's dismal, " Yes "to hat's bright; " No " to what's folly, and " Yes" to what's wise; Seeking to see things with spirit-cleansed eyes. '• No " to all error, and " Yes "to what's true, Trusting the truth wlll bring me safe through ; "Yes" to what's manly, and "No " to what's mean ; "No "to what soils me, and " Yes" t what's clean; "No " to what's cowardly, " Yes " to what's brave ; Eager to help men—eager to sive. "No " to what crushes, and " Yes " to what cheers; " Yes" to glad hopes, and " No " to sad fears ; " No " to all worry, and " Yes " to hard work ; " Yes "to plain duty not once may I shirk. "Yes "to what's honest and noble and pure; " No to what's doubtful, however secure; " No " to ungentleness, " Yes" to love; " Yes" to all good things that come from above.

POLICE AND PUBLIC.

STRANGE CONFLICT OF EVIDENCE AT WIDNES At Widnes, on Monday, William Handley, Ross-street engine-driver, was charged with being drunk on Saturday night. It was stated that accused, at 6 15, as F Gen by Sergeant Waters,n and a constable near the Central Hotel, Victoria-road, to stagger about, and he was refused admission on the motor-bus.

The Magistiatta' Clerk : Are you sure about his condition ?

Sergeant Waterson : I have not the slightest doubt as to his being drunk. Councillor Lewis intimated that he had seen the affair, and left the bench for the witness-box. He gave evidence that be was in the neighbourhood on the night, in question. He noticed a man whom be believed to be Handley standing quietly en the opposite side of the road, as mentioned by the police. Two officers approached the man and entered into conversation, and later he saw the three walk to the police station. There was no sign of drink on the man,and he did not stagger in any way. Witness wondered what the police would charge him with. He had beard the evidence of the police, and he had received a very painful impression. The Superintendent: Was it that man ? Councillor Lewis : I cmnot identify his features. Major Wareing: As there is a conflict of evidence, the case is dismissed.

The Superintendent : I'm sorry ! I'm sorry ! It is a very Eerious matter when there is direct evidence from three sergeants and two constables. lam sorry you don't believe their word, and take the word of Is witness who can't swear to him.

"THE HOUSE THAT JACK BUILT."

NURSERY RHYME IN ARBITRATION CASE'.

THE CASE FOR THE CORPORATION.

One does not usually look to arbitration proceed- ings for humorous interludes, but there were several amusing touches in the curse of the final day's hear- ing of the arbitration concerning the claim of Messrs James Fsirclough and Sons, Ltd., against the War- rington Corporation on Saturday. Mr Gordon Hewart KU., who represented the Corporation, treated the court to an adaptation of "The House that Jack built." He was referring to the contention of the claimants that profits made at their mills in respect of orders received at the Bridge Foot warehouse, which was the subject of arbitration, should be included in the estimate of the loss of business cau,ed by the demolition of the latter building to allow of the erection of the new Warrington Bridge. The lines were :

This is the warehouse at Bridge Foot. This is the flour and these are the oats That are sold at the warehouse at Bridge Foot And this is the miller of Mersey Mill, ho grinds the corn that makes the flour That's sold at the warehouse at Bridge Foot. To carry the argument further : And this is the farmer, now forlorn, Who somewhere in Cheshire grows the corn 'I hat the Mersey miller grinds into flour To he sold at the warehouse at Bridge Foot.

In his address to the Court, Mr Hewart said that in a case where an individual was making a claim against the Corporation, one expected exaggeration, indeed great exaggeration, but he respectfully submittei that in this Case the etaggeration had been a little overdone It had been a little unusually great. Vhat was the position ? They were told in one breath that the fair way of looking at the land and buildings was to tate them as they were. The claimant was to have the bentfit, whatever it might be, on that mode of valua- tion. Vtien they came to dent with the vacant land, however, that idea was abandoned at the same time that it, was maintained. Ilut take the buildings, as they were. The warehouse which had been carried on for all these years, and with all the developments which had been so eloquently pictured, was makirig a profit of £224 per year on a turnover of £lO,OOO. Like the fairy in the story they said in regard to these euildinga "I am not what I am, bur what I shall Le," rbey had been invited to put themselves into the ate mines of a r Faircleugh, his brother and his fatter at the time when, fifty yearn ago, they entered lipen those premises. That really went to the root of the claim as putt forward. They went to those premises, so they were told, because it was in their minds, greatly to cevelr p them .reci the primary considerittion which influenced their minas in toe choice of Lhat site, the element they looked forward to for the great development, which was going to cause such an immense saving in railway traffic, was access to the river and the use of river transport. What happened ? I hey made a sort of lending stage and as soon as they had brought two or three barges down there, about the sear 1863,they made the disco‘ery that their warehouse, as it was, would not hold a barge load. Here were those gentlemen who, at that time, were full of the notion of developing that warehouse and making use ot the river as a great instru- ment in Re development, but the moment they used even the existing facilities of the river they found they were too great for the existing warehouse. What did they do ? Add to it the saddlers shop next door, which was fetching a rent of .05 a sear, and probably less at that time ?—Not at all. What they did was to abandon the scheme of development and river transport and SIT DOWN FOR 50 YEARS

with their warehouse as it was and make no use over of of that which, according to their statements, had mainly operated upon their minds in the choice of site. Dealing with the reasons advanced by Messrs Fairclough as to why they had not developed the warehouse, Mr Hewart said that while the Warrington Bridge improve- ment was in contemplation, Messrs Fairclough stated that they were restrained, by a sense of delicacy, from' incurring any expenditure which would have swollen the claim they would have to make against the the Warrington Corporation. He (Mr Hewart), had only one remark to make about that, the gentleman on whose behalf that was stated, when the day came to make a claim against the Corporation, claimed £6,900 in a matter in which his valuers, when in the box, could at the utmost stretch, ask only .£I,BCO. Was not the proof of this pudding in the eating ? The profit on the warehouse represented 2 per cent on the turnoYer and it was suggested that the businesrewas so sensitive a plant that even the slightest removal would immedi- ately extinguish it. Tnat was the business and those were the premises for which the arbitrators were asked to give that large sum of money, not because of what it was, less because of what it bad been but because of what was called the prospective value of the property and its potential facilities Dealing with the sugges- tion of the claimants' valuers that the buildings were to he valued as they stood,but that the land at the back was to be valued as if the buildings had been pulled down. Mr Hewart said the claimants

WANTED TO EAT THEIR CAKE AND TO HAVE IT as well. Alluding to the contention that the river transport could be developed, Mr liewart said the firm would have to spend £3OO or £4OO on dredging in order to command aovantages which might be etstimated at £2OO to £4OO. Moreover, the engineering difficulties in the way of constructing a wharf were so great as to be absolutely insuperable. His learned friend had referred to the introduction of what he called comic opera into the case but he, (Mr Hewart) submitted that that term might aptly be applied to Messrs Fairclough's claim. It was suggested that the farmers who came into Warrington would be deterred from doing business with Met sra Fairclough because they removed their premises to a street which happened to be out of their line of route. Was ever a more fantastic suggestion made to a serious tribunal ? So desperate was the position that Mes.rs Fairclough represented to be that it was not even worth their while to look out for alternative premises. Time would show—he would be interested to see three or four years from now whether that was really the case. Mr Hewart concluded by submitting that £2,800 would be ample compensation for the property, land and business.

Mr Sanderson K.C.in his reply submitted that if there had been any exaggeration it had been in the way in which the Corporation had approached the case. The previous day he was perfectly justified in stating that some of the questions that his learned friend was putting to witnesses were worthy of nothing but

COMIC OPERA. , Apparently his learned friend had been burning mid- night oil -he agreed with great success, for he knew his skill at that kind of thing—to fit the comic opera with the libretto which he had 10,-en reading to tuem that day. But that was a business matter and they were not going to treat it from the point of view of comic) opera. If tho Corporation had agreed to give Messrs Fairclough access to the river, that arbitration would, in all probability, never have been heard of. There was nothing more unreasonable than the arguments whick had been brought against Messrs Fbirclough. They all knew Mr Fairclough was a well- known man in Warrington. Everybody had been saying what a bigh reputation he had. His brother had been Mayor of the borough, and Mr Fairclough would be the last man in the world who would put an extra burden on the Corporation if he could help it. Mr tianderson submitted (1) the site with regard to the road was most important ; (2) to Messrs Fairclough it bore an enhanced value because it had access to the river ; (3) because the river could and would be used if Messrs Fairclough were only left in possession of tbe property. The real importance of the case was not the present value, but the potential value of the site. The earlier part of the day was devoted to hearing witnesses on behalf of the Corporation, Mr James Henry Sephton, valuer, described the posi- tion of t!,.e warehouse as a most dangerous one, and pointed out that there was only room for one cart to stand between the tramway and the footpath, therefore any cart that was stopped, loading at the ware- house, was blocking the whole stream of traffic Moreover,in emptying and loading carts they had to be extremely careful of the pedestrians passing, or the bags would fall on therm No use whatever was made of the river on the south side between the Warrington Bridge end the weir, a distance of one third of a mile. If the river access bad been regarded as valuable one would have expected the south bank to be used for business purposes but as it was it was occupied by cottages. The warehouse itself was an adapted tool and a very badly adapted One. It was both unsuitable in its structure and in its convenience. He did not con- sider Messrs Fairclough would suffer any loss at all by removal of the business, as there were suitable premises about 130 yards distant. The full valuation which he placed on the property including the expesse of removal was £2,000. Mr Wiliam Henry Hunter, late chief consulting engineer to the Manchester ship Canal Company said that having regard to tbe cost of development and the engineering difficulties, which he regarded as insuper- able, he did not place any advantage on the river front- age from the standpoint of access and traflio. His valuation of the property was £l,BOl 2s. The ware- house, he said, was in a precarious condition and a severe storm might wreck the gable end overlooking the river.

Mr Sanderson: There vvas an exceedingly teve,e storm yesterday week. Witness : Yes.

Have you impeded the premises since ?—No. Have you heard they have sustained any damage ? No I should have heard if they had. They have withstood about as severe a storm as was ever known in this neighbourhood then ?—I won't admit that.

Witness added that the damage would depend on the quarter from which the storm came.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Court Bouquet.—Fewer flowers were carried at the Courts last year than ever before, but this year has seen the revival of the graceful "shower" bouquet. The florists doubtless rejoice at the turn of fashion's wheel also the nervous debutante who frequently finds diffi- culty in occupying her hands. As in the case of bridal array, a " shower "of t.looms and foliage, adds much to the beauty of a Court toilette, and is of great assist- ance in recovering balance, after the deep obeisance which is now required. Queen Alexanara.—Fifty years ago Queen Alexandra was busy making preparations for her marriage, it was on March 7th, 1863, that tbe "sea-king's daughter, from over the seas " arrived in British waters and three days later she was wedded to the Prince of Wales—the late Kieg Edward, amid scenes of great splendour in St. George's Chapel, Windsor. It is a long time to reflect over, and must bring to the mind of the widowed Queen memories of many joys and sorrows when she looks back over her fifty years' residence in this country. From the first she won all hearts by her grace and sweetness of disposition and has ever retained the high esteem and deep affections of the nation. She was Princess of Wales for thirty- seven years, and both as Princess and Queen, she has devoted her life to helping worthy objects and the furtherance of philanthropic work. It is right and proper that the nation should celebrate in a fitting manner Queen Alexandra's Jubilee anniversary. Alexandra Day.—Later on it is proposed to repeat the great floral fete inaugurated last year, and known as Alexandra Day. Oa that. occasion some millions of little artificial floral emblems were sold in the streets of London by lady flower sellers of all ranks in life, for the ben4llt of deserving charities. 'lbis year tha movement will be widely taken up in the provinces,and in a large number of towns preparations ara already being made to celebrate Alexandra Day on June 25th next, the benefit in these inetancem going to local charities. The central offices of the Alexandra Day celebration, are at 10, West Bolton Gardens, South Kensingten, London, from whence all information may be obtained.

The Lady Emigrant.—There are not a few gentle- women, who, finding little opportanities for a career in the old country, are turning their thoughts to the Colonies. Many who have been tronght up with no special training and no definite cr,ject in life, are not the best fitted for Colonial life, hut the practical capable %omen, aith knowledge end experience in business and honsehold matters, is pretty sure to eo well in Ca ads, or Alortrolia. She can at all t=es con- fidently rvly on her own resources, but others nho nuty be thiolcing of going West, and are not so sure of them- selves, night obtain some gnidanoe and assistance from the Queen Mary's C.r..nation Hotel which has just been op.,neci at Vane over for " blentlevromen born in the United litngd,ru seeking employment in British Columbia." The main object which this instituti“n has in view is to afford pupils and residents praotioal train- ing in household word and life, as it exists in that vast and progressive province. The lady emigrant who goes out to Canada must, of conrse, adapt herself to the ways and ideas of the country rind it, is for teaching her " local conditions " that the hotel at Vancouver has been established.

Housecraft.—Educational authorities continue to give more and more attention to housecraft, as a regular school subject. This is as it should be, for science teaching in this form is of practical use when school- days are done, which cannot be said of much of the present day teaching in a generally overcrowded curriculum. in Lonoon the County Council is pursuing an active policy in the teaching of housewifery subjects and will at Easter open a new domestic economy school at Neacemen-street, Borough, when twenty-five scholarships vvill be awarded to girls between fourteen and sixteen. The otjeet of the school will be to furnieh a preparation for domestic service on a scientific basis, and a continuation of general education. Some Materials.—That there is nothing new under the sun is proved every day, and each season some old friend returns to us in a different guise. This spring a fabric appears that closely resembles the rep, of perhaps thirty years ago. It is, however, a glorified edition of its old self, and being very handsome in appearance will find favour for coats and skirts of the more expensive varieties. Another fabric that seems to have charmed tailors and dressmakers alike, is a new silk corduroy, in which the rib is neither heavy nor pronounced, but ;rather perhaps suggested. It is as pliable and soft as charmeuse, cr ninon, and lends itself to the draped styles of the moment, as well as to the more eevere modes. In old-fashioned fawn colour, with a fine black hair line, it is particularly pleasing in a tailored suit, trimmed with white matalasse, a material favoured this spring for collars and revers on suits of simple make, 'lbis Season's Novelties.—The new draped skirts are among the most noteworthy novelties that have far appeared. They are marvels of manipulation, and it is in their draping that the character of these dresses must be sougbt. Some models have the fulness brought towards the front, the hips being quite plain. It is said in Paris that there will be no radical changes in styles tots summer. Changes there will be, of course, but these will effect the draperies rather than anything else. Styles that preserve the slimness of line, and accentu- ate length, will still prevail, which styles are by no means adapted to stout figures, and corsetieres are endeavouring to design models that have the effect of reducing superabundance of sditipose tissue. Some of the newest coats, to be worn with skirts that match them, resemble in cut a man's morning coat. They are very slightly full at the back, and this fulness is con- fined by means of a strap. Thus the resemblance mentioned is in the style of the front, not of the back. The waist coat is en important accessory, and has made its appearance rather perhaps in the guise of a vest on smart afternoon dresses. The Newest Shirts.—Buttons are still a very important item of dress, and are used on the newest white satin or cotton crepe shirts, worn with the fashionable white cloth dresses to give a welcome touch of colour. Cotton crepe is a material both useful and smart, and most suitable for the severe shirt of this year. The new buttons are highly decorative,evpecially those Wade of amber, lapis lazuli, and onyx. These are generally shaped liked marbles, and are placed down the front of the shirt in little groups, some distance apart. Small speckled buttons, sometimes pink and white, reminiscent of hundreds and thousands, or in bl,ue and green, are also used. These newest shirts are devoid of tucks or any trimming beyond buttons, their shape is has defined than last year, and they bang somewhat limply. They are usually made with an open collar, which is turned back and either hemstitched at the edge or finished with French knots.

Dainty and Becoming Veilings.—For ceremonious occasions the large hat will come into its own again, as Spring advances. And with the lnrge hat will come the veil, and the new veilings leave little to be desired. Gone are the heavy unbecoming varieties. Gone are the hideous spiders In their webs—a one-time favourite design, and which turned the prettiest girl into a dis- figured monster. The new shadow veilings are delightful and highly becoming, and the girl or woman who fails to find something in this direction to please her, must sirely be a most discontented human being.

DEATH OF THE BISHOP'S BROTHER.

The death took place suddenly from heart failure of Sir Thomas Frederick Chavasse, the eminent Midland surgeon, at his residence near Bromsgrove.Birminglrim. Sir Thomas, who was a brother of the Bishop of Liverpool, held a distinguished position in his pro- frssion. The son of a doctor who was well known in Warwickshire, the deceased was n Fellow of the Royal College of Surgeons. He studied at the Universities of Edinburgh, Vienna, and Berlin, and was n valued con- tributor to the literature of his profession. Among medical men in the city of Birmingham be held an eminent p,sition,and was a justice of the peace for the county of Warwick.

IF cutting his teeth makes your child fretful, get a Steedman's Powder for him. This medicine contains no poison or narcotic, but relieves feverish heat and preserves a healthy state of the constitution.

DISPARAGING TERRITORIALS.

MR. A. H. CROSFIELD AND THE CONSCRIPTIONISTS.

CLuncillor Peter Peacock, chairman of the Execu- tive Committee of the Liberal Association, entertained the members of the Liberal Committee of Howley Ward at dinner at Atkinson's Cafe on Friday evening of last week. There was a large gathering presided over by Mr W Clegg (chairman of the ward committee), and a very hearty reception was accorded to Mr A H Crosfield who delivered an address full of encourage- to the wot kers. The other speakers included Mr C E Parker (president of the Liberal Association), Councillor Peacock and Mr M Llewellyn. Mr CROSFIELD expressed the appreciation of all who were aotively associated with the Liberal party in ‘A'arringtoti of the consistently active work which Councillor Peacock threw into the cause. f Applause.) Having referred to the tough light which confronted the ward committee in gaining the ascendancy of Liberalism in the Howley Ward, Mr Crostleld said the policy for which they had to fight was such a splendid one, that he did not doubt their ability to put the Liberal flag at the top of the mast, if they devoted the necessary time and energy to the task. The contrast between the work done in Parliament by the friends of sections and privileges, when they had the power, and the present democratic Liberal and progressive adminis- tration was so strikingly complete, that if he had the opportunity of getting face to face with any waverers —always assuming that they were men of common- sense—he would have good hopes, in ninety-nine cases out of a hundred, of making a convert to the Liberal side. (Applause.)Not only would he emphasise the work which the Liberals were doing, but he would ask what sort of an account their opponents had to give of themselves. After having spent a mint of money in England in the last decade, in engineering the " raging and tearing" propaganda, the tariff reformers found themselves to-day uncertain whether they were on their heads or their heels, with NO INTF.LLIGIBLE POLICY, with nothing hut contradictioris from day to day—Mr Boner Law going to Edinburgh and saying one thing, Lord Derby coming to Lancashire and saying another, Mr Austen Chamberlain another, and Mr Chaplin another. It was indeed a sorry spectacle for a once great party to present. (Hear, hear.) If the people of this country had not scotched Protection—which they were presently going to kill outright—(h(ar, hear)— the sacrifices which would have been demanded by the mess.-13 as time went on, would have been measured in not hing less than shillings a week as a reduction of wages. Mr Crosfield next touched upon the great work which the Ow eminent had done for the people in passing the Old Age Pensions and Natinal Insurance Acts, referring to the effect which the latter must inevitably have on health and le using and the q iestien of the minimum wege. Ile admitted that the latter proposition raised difficult ano complicated issues, hut it was no more to be shin ked by responsible statesmen on that ace, unt. While he was in Perliarurnt, end since his fellow-towinimerein a moment of abberationohought fit to relegate him to a position of greater freedom and less responsibility—(laughter)---he had been much engrossed in the question of the hours of labour, chirtly as it affected our own populatioe, but also as it concerned industrial communities abroad. In this connection Mr Crosfleld alluded to his active interest in the work of the International Association for Labour Legislation, and emphasised the fact that the far-reaching reforms which it had already been able to secure, were the outcome of inter- national co-operation. It was now engaged on the larger task of getting rid of the old, evil and discredited system of long working days of ten or twelve hours, and he was thankful to assure them that the feeling in favour of the eight hcurs' day, which was making such splendid headway in many industries in England, MIS not confined to this country. International co-opera- tion was a floe thing from the industrial point of view, and he valued it not less because of the

SANE INTERNATIONALISM which it developed as a substitute for the extreme follies of those who wanted to exaggeste international hatreds and lead us into an extravagance of militarism which he for one would never support. (Applause.) Mr Crosfielei emphasised the N. alue of the concession of greater leisure to the workers, especially in its effect upon all branches of public activity. Would it not,for instance, be very much easier to get all the support we wanted for our Territorials, if men enjoyed reasonable leisure, and if officers and men alike were not penalized for serving in the force? He had frequently discussed that aspect of the question with Lord Haldane and had urged that, so far as he (Mr Crosfield) was concerned, he would not stint expenditure upon the Territorial force which would enable officers and men to servo without being penalized for doing so. (Applause.) It might be advisable to go a step further and give some little merit hedge or mark of honour to a man who had voluntarily served his country in that way. That distinction might be taken into account by all our public authorities when giving employment and it would undoubtedly be considered by many private employers. If we gave our population a fair amount of leisure, coupled with good, vigorous alert work for eight hours a day,he did not believe we were so lacking in public spirit in England that there would he any shortage in the Territorials. At present there was a shortage of 50,000 or 60,000, but when he considered the discouragemeiats under which the Territorials laboured, the wonder to him was that the deficiency was not greater. (Hear, heir.) lie had the utmost admires tion for their work, which he hoped would receive the honour it deserved, and he deprecated the AMAZING CAMPAIGN OF DISPARAGEMENT of the force which was at present going on throughout the country. The way in which it had been organized and engineered in the Press was no credit to those who we ire at the back of t. (A Voice: "The National Service League has something to do with it.") He would rather not dwell upon that point, though he agreed with his friend that the League bad a pretty heavy responsibility to reckon with. At any rate, he thought that campaign had gone quite far enough. Mr Crosfield recalled the saying of Rohn Burns, n few years ago, that we were faced with "the Jingo rake's conspiracy Protection, conscription and slavery." Chinese labour —which he (the speaker) had always described as quasi- slavery—had been swept out of South Africa,, and Protection would soon receive its death blow.. . 'there was there was that other wretched remnant which was raising its feeble head—the remains of the hydra—conscription— but he thought it had a very poor chance in this country. At the same time, they had to see to it that they did not allow any such folly to make undue head- way. (Applause.) They all respected Lord Roberts as a warrior—(hear, hear)—but that was no reason why they should ask the warrior to take the place of the statesman who was responsible for our policy. Mr Crosfield quoted the following from the speech of Lord Haldane in the House of Lords debate the previous Tuesday : "If we aimed at raising the only kind of home defence army which would put us on anything like the Continental level we must dismiss all hope of being able to fulfil our first duty—that for pro- viding the oversee army and the expeditionary force which the Empire required. Five out of six soldiers agreed with the principle of com- pulsory service, but there the agreement ended and discord began. In the second part of Lord Roberts's book Fancies and Facts, the writer whom the noble Earl asked to write that part started with the necessity of maintaining the two-power standard of naval strength, putting the Navy Estimates alone at from £60,000,000 to £80,000,000. Next, the writer said we required the ' oversee garrisons of to-day and a small mobilized reserva to support them. Next, we wanted an army for home defence to consist of 300,000 men, to be expanded by reserves to 500,000. Finally behind that, army ye wanted an extra force of 500,000 trained for six months. Such a plan would have uevesal consequences. First, it would make us so formidable a nation that other nations would huddle togetber for safety, and instead of , friendship we should find suspicion. Secondly, the country would speedily be bankrupt. Thirdly, the Government which proposed the plan would be cast out of power by an indignant nation." That, continued Mr Crosfield, was the criticism of very moderate man and he would ask the attention of the Liberals of Warrington to the length to which Lord Roberts, the head of the National Service Leaque, went in his demands upon the country. There was an old saying in Lancashire" Eating is like fighting, it only wants begining," and the appetite of the advocates of compulsory service had grown since they first brought forward their proposals. They as Liberals did not want that kind of thing and they did not intend—indeed they had no fear that the Government would commit itself to such a piece of folly which .could assuredly turn it out of power. In conclusion, Mr Crosfield said that, speaking impersonally, he hoped all the workers present would do their utmost to see that the political complexion of Warrrington again became what it ought to be, in an industrial centre where the lives of so many of the workingclass population were vitally affected by the good government of the country. (Applause).

WESTERN PRESPERITY AND AGRICULTURE. In round figures says the " Monetary Times " (Toronto), the grain crop of Canada's prairie provinces is worth 52 millions sterling. That is nearly .£4O for every man, woman and child in that region. The wheat would make flour to feed for one year the entire popula- tion of England, Ireland And Scotland, and take care of the Dominion besides. From the proceeds of the oat crop, one could hand every person in the Dominion a ten-dollar bill MO. The barley and flax crops weuld fill 49,000 large sized box cars. To move this entire grain crop requires 473,000 cars, equal to a train reach- ing from Montreal to Vancouver, and enough cars left over to make another smaller train reacbitig from Winipeg to Calgary. These oars would be equivalent to 11,825 regular sized trains, and if they passed through any given town in Canada, one behind the other, at thirty miles on hour, one would have to stand on the station platform six days and six nights to see them pass by.
