Memorial Service in Queen's Gardens.

On Sunday, the 2nd March, a short service will be held, by kind permission of the Parks Committee, in front of the Regimental Memorial in the Queen's Gardens at Warrington, The service, which will commence at 12 30 p.m. and conclude about 12.45 p.m., is in memory of those office's, non-commissioned officers and men of all the Bitttalions of the Regiment who gave their lives for their country during the South African War of 1899-1902. It will be attended by the men of the Ist, 2nd and 3td Battalions who are now serving at the Depot in Orford Barracks and by the Recruits flow under to aining there. Colonel 13 Faitclough,commanding the 4th Battalion (late the Ist Volunteer Battalion) and Colonel L E Pilkington, commanding the sth Battalion (late the 2nd Volunteer battalion) have kindly given permission for detachments of their Battalions to be present and the band of the 4th Battalion, with the buglers from the depot, will perform the musical portion of the service.

A short address will be given by the Rev W Bracecatnp, chaplain of the. 4th Battalion, who will conduct the service.

During the service, wreaths from the variou battalions in memory of their comrades who were killed or died in the war, will be placed upon the memorial and it is hoped that any of the public who lost relatives or friends will take the opportunity of placing any tribute they may wish round the memorial.

The troops will be in position by 12.25 and the public are requested not to enter the gardens until after the troops have taken up their position.

deity, the hideous " goddess of getting on." Right to the very heart of the thing Ruskin struck, condemning, root and branch, a civilisation in which work was done only for profit, measured by money, wbere wealth and riches meant only so much stored coin of the realm, mere devilish dust, and never the happiness of men and women and the innocent joy in labour. They knew how he attacked with terrific scorn and contempt the ruling notions of political economy and transferred t-) Gehenna every acc•epted idea of the meaning of value and wealth. His friends begged him to be calm and to moderate the force of his expressions —but no ! "Those expressions " he wrote " may do me harm or do me . good, what is that to me? They are the only true, right, or possible expressions. The science of political economy is a lie—wholly and to the root (as hitherto taught.) It is also the damnedest, that the devil or betrayer of men has yet invented except the Devil's theory of sanctification. To this science' and .to this alone (the professed and organised pursuit of money) is owing all the evil of modern days. I say all. It is now the money theory which corrupts the church, corrupts the household life, destroys honour, beauty and life throughout the universe." They should remember that that theory of political economy, that organised following of money, was the reflection of actual life and still, to this iOUr, the basis of our social system and they would see how, for Ruskin, and for all right seeing men, there was written across the whole face of our political and social life the words " This is a lie and a sham" !. They should bring all their reckonings in social orgrisation down to the terms of the actual living and immortal souls of men and women to measure all things by the smiles and tears of their fellows in God. Grasp hut the fringe of Ruskin's teaching and they would have set about the destruction of the present order and the building of a new one.

ANTARCTIC HEROES.

Pulpit Tributes to Captain Scott and his

Comrades.

References to the Antarctic disaster was made at several places of worship on Sunday. Preaching at Golhorne-street Baptist Church, in the evening the Rev H Ogden said all were moved by the bravery of men who for no personal gain had pushed out the frontiers of human knowledge, but had paid for it with their lives. Soon, possibly, there might he churlish enough ito inquire : " Why all this waste ? What does t profit when the vole is discovered ? Why should is brave man and his brave wife surrender £30,000 of their personal estate and their conjugal happiness to follow a the-Wisp' ? " But just now they remembered the bravery of Englishmen and Britishers, the widows and the fatherless, and might Captain Scott's last message burn into the country's pockets so that the dependents of the heroes might not suffer. One lesson they were taught by this tragic chapter in Antarctic di,coveries was that Nature had her own surprises. She gave up her secrets tardily and very unwillingly, CaWain Scott, even with disaster cr?,rtaiti around hi m still believed in God. He demonstrated that this age as all others, was hungry for courageous enterprise. eople suffered to-day from nests that were made, and if not silver spoons, nickel-plated ones. It was hardly edifying to watch brawny young men rubbing grime from the corner of some corporation building. An old clout would do the work more efficiently. Young men with no nobler use for their leisure than lounging put their mothers to unnecessary trouble. They went through pain without a purpose. Captain Scott's enter- prise was brave and heroic. There was a vision in it. It was an cinterprise for the good of man." ." Peace bath her greater triumphs than war.' England rejoiced in the attitude of her beloved King. Such monarchs transformed Republicans into Monarchists. They honoured no man because he was his father's son, but for what he was, and that message to the mother of Captain Scott was a national treasure. Not as a king did be go to St Paul's,hut as a colleague. He remembered Captain Scott as a friend. Some dog would growl even about the King's thoughtfulness, but dogs had barking threats. All Englishmen shared in the common grief. The widow on the high sea., and the widow in her cottage in Wales were equally precious to God and the people. Mrs Soott would suffer no more than the widow of Petty Officer Evans. The mustval ue tirne at ldi.etihr ep.wr aiyneerpsr. e 8 sE va el or ny em, ba un lainsdtenevteory chin:hap were equally precious. They were of equal vvcrds of aptain Scott : " These rough notes and our thewo last dead bodies must tell the tale, but surely, surely a great, rich country like ours will see that those whO'are dependent upon us are properly provided for." Prayer without works was dead. Warrington could help. The Mayor might inaugurate a fund, and as far as rnoney could, the town could pay a tribute to the memories of brave British explorers. REV. T. T. JAMES'S REFERENCE.

Preaching at Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday morning, the Rev T. T James took for his subject " The sword of the wilderness, " hir.3 text being " We get cur bread with the peril of our lives because of the sword of the wilderness " (Lamentations v., 9). He said it was only now and then that a 9 event touched the whole heart ot England as the heroic story of the South Pole exploration ptrty had done that week.. It, had thrilled a nation. Beyond the bounds of English- speaking people, it had spoken to the common heart of humanity. The brave men whose daring and whose SaCCiao9' had been published in all ears were on no mere quest of adventure. Doubtless the witching call of sheer venturesome exploits had been beard by them, and they went on with that absolute delight which hazardous undertakings possessed for human nature. But their task was also part of the world's work ; it was to advance our knowledge, to render service to science, to bring nearer to completion our underatanding of the physical world which we inhabited. The fruits of that enterprise were preserved through all d inger to life itself. In the stress of direat difficulty the treasure of discovery was never sur- rendered ; even geological specimens were held and carried when their forsaking might have increased the chance of personal deliverance : even in the face of death itself records were written which would be an enrichment of the knowledge of mankind. Tnis was no mere adventure, it was part of the world's work, and as we honoured these men to-dray who across the wide expanse of sea sailed in their little craft, and who across untrodden fields of ice pursued their goal with unshaken courage and came at list to " Sleeps after toyle, port after stormie seas," we knew that it was work that never lacked the element of peril from beginning to end. "We get our bread with tee peril of our lives, because of the sword of the wilderness." And it was easy to imagine that this was something extraordinary when it was nothing of the kind. Doubtless the heroic and dramatic story which had stirred our blood was extraordinary enough, but the thing itself—that the work of the world was done often enough with the peril of our lives—that was commonplace, so commonplace that we often forgot it. In many cf the simple necessities without which our homes would not hold together for a week, we were dealing with the fruits of labour that always had upon it the mark of danger risked and not infrequently of s. orifice actually made. There were blue books full of the reports.of Royal Commissions that had sat to dis- cuss the problems of dangerous occupations. There were hospitals where the lead and steel and other workers of our country daily poured their stock of con- sumptive patients. The coal we burnt this day at home had had its history set many a time in lurid colours. And there were parts of the world's work, eqqally humble though more picturesque, which demeneed a permanent faciug of the dire dangers which threatened life itself.

DEATH OF AIRS. THOMAS GLOVER.

e;ach regret is felt in St Helens at the news of the death on Sunday, of Mrs Fanny Glover, widow of tho late Mr Thomas Glover,ex-Labour M.P. for the borough and treasurer of the Lancashire and Cheshire Miners' Federation.

The hand of death has indeed fallen heavily on the family, for within the last throe years, besides Mr and Mrs Glover, two daughters have passed away. Mrs Glover, who was 62 years of age, and came from Birmingham, had been an invalid for some time, and her end, which came suddenly from heart failure, was not expected. She was married to Mr Glover in 1872, and assisted him greatly in his arduous work as a miners' leader. For some time she was treasurer of th 3 St Helens branch of the Women's Labour League. Three sons and two daughters are left. The funeral will take place at St Helens Cemetery on Thursday.

SOCIALISTS OPPOSE NEW YACHT FOR KAISER, In the Budget Committee of the Reichstag on Wed- nesday the Socialists opposed a vote for a new Imperial yacht which is to replace the Hohenzollern, alleging that the ship was useless for war purposes, and that if the Emperor needed a yacht he should pay for it him- self.

The Examiner, Saturday, February 22, 1913,

TILE DEFORESTATION OF LYII M.—About twelve months ago many inhabitants of Lymm noticed with regret that several of the fine trees in the Dingle had been felled; this distruction was caused by the shortage of fuel at the Waterworks owing to the memorable coal strike. However, though fuel is more plentiful at present the cutting down of trees is continued, happily not in the Dingle, but chiefly in the district round Oughtrington Church. Up to last autumn this was regarded as one of the prettiest spots in the neighbour- hood, but now its beauty is largely gone, the trees that used to be dotted up on.l down the fields, and the wood at the back of Messrs Th,omason's shop have disappeared Even the once almost sacrosant Ellesdale wood bas been entered and many of its giants brought to the ground destroying much of the charm that has invested it for so many years. Deansgreen, Crouchley, and Massy Brook districts have also been visited by the woodmae with his axe and saw, with the result that, in all, including the trees that have been cut down to make way for the new road, Lymm has lost quite two hundred trees during the past six months. We suppose we must not call this vandalism, but Lila sacrifice of beauty on the altar of commerce.

A LOCAL EMIGRANT.—On Wednesday evening the members of the Wesleyan Choir providea a most enjoy- able social as a send-off to Mr Leonard Waterworth who sails for Canada at the end of this month. The school- room had been tastefully adorned for the occasion and many favourable comments were passed by the large number of persons present. The proceedings, which were presided over by Mr H S Brown, organist and choirmaster, were opened by a cleverly played piano- forte duet, by Miss Rogerson, and Mr G Smith, who gave another item at a later stage. The choir sang tw, glees, and Sidney Wright two solos. The chief item of the evening was the pre- sentation of a travelling clock to Mr L Waterworth which Mr H S Brown mach, on behalf of the choir. Mr Brown spoke in glowing terms of Mr L Water- worth's services as Librarian, and a member of the choir and expressed his wishes for his future welfare. A suitable reply was wade by the recipient. The rest of the evening was devoted to a hearty participation in the c,qmpPtitive games that had been arranged. Lighting candles, won by Miss Winstanley ; drawing competition, won by Miss Mairs ; guessing competition, won by Miss Winstanley's party ; winking, blindfold jumping, guessing the tune, Sc. Choice refreshments were served by some of the choir members the Misses Egerton Hazelhurst, Pearson, Rogerson, M Winstanley and E Winstanley.

MISSIONARY ANstvErtsA [vv.—The Home and Foreign Missionary anniversary of the Primitive Methodist Church was observed on Sunday. The preacher was Mr H S Gerrard, of Hartley CAlege, Manchester, who is at present concluding his studies preparatory to taking up Me(lical lissionsry work in Africa. At the morning service the scholars of the morning school sang a Missionary collecting hyma. At this service Mr Gerrard spoke on the words" I made all things to all men, that I might by all means SWO some," and showed that to be successful in Christian labours the worker must adapt himself to the varied conditions of the work. The Missionary must therefore have a knowledge of the language, hibits, nr)de of living and religions needs of the people he goes to. lie described the value of medical missionary work and said that Christianity must be the power behind all efforts. Mr Gerrard gave an address in the Sunday school in the afternoon, and at night preached from Acts xiv. 27. Speaking of the recently "opened doors" of Africa, Japan and China, the preacher said that though the advantages of civilization which the West had taken into these countries were great, set many evils had also been introduced, and that it became the added duty of the Church to remove these wrongs. He said that the present age was a missionary one and that the outstanding word in the first volume of the report of the 1910 Edinburgh Conference was " Urgency." He pleaded for a generous support of the missionary cause. The choir rendered the anthem " Great and mar- vellous " (Terrier).

The annual Missionary meeting was held in the school room on Monday night when the Rev W Chap- man, of Nambala. was the deputation. 'Mr J D B Smith, the chairman spoke of the pleasure it gave him to be at the meeting and said that we were indebted to the British and Foreign Bible Society, and Foreign Missionary Societies for the great work they did. He expressed his surprise that some people did not believe in supporting them. He spoke appreciatively of Mr Chapman's entrancing book " A Pathfinder in South Central Africa "and said that if only some of these critics of Missionary work would read it he was sure that their false opinions would be changed. The Rev W Chapman gave a delightful and instructive lecture on his pioneer work among the native tribes of South Central Africa, describing the modes of travel, natural history, native life, the building of, and work on the Mission Stations. The lecture was enriched by the exhibition of a large number of original slides, shown by Mr Moss. In making the financial statement, the Rev R W B Whitewav said that he was pleased to report an increase on last year's result. He proposed a vote of thanks to the Rev W Chapman, Mr J D B Smith, and the following collectors, Mrs Edwards,Miss Hallam, Marion Evans. Eva Tonge,and [Tarry Cheetham. This WAS seconded by Mr G L Welford and passed with acclamation. Mr Smith replied briefly, after which the Doxology was sung and the Benediction pronounced. The attendance at each of the services was very good.

Borough Police Court

WEDNESDAY.—Before Mr J W Hayward (in the chair) Alderman Smethurst, Messrs C Jackson Holmes and

W T Williams. THEFT BY A LODGER.—Joseph Reuben Hodson, of no fixed abode, was charged with having stolen an overcoat, the property of Robert Connor, on January 25th, and also with housebreaking on February Ist. Chief Inspector Williamson said the prisoner had been lodging with Connor for a few weeks, and on January 25th he took the prosecutor's overcoat from behind the door. Connor asked him to leave it alone hut prisoner said he would return in a few minutes. He was not seen again by the prosecutor. On February Ist, early in the morning, the prosecutor heard someone opening the front door but thought it was a lodger going out. When he got up he found his trousers were missing from the bedroom and in the pockets was a sum of money. He also missed several articles of clothing. Connor found that the front door key had been taken from underneath the door. It was his practice to leave it there and the prisoner was aware of that feet. Prisoner was apprehended at St Helens on the 11th inst. and admitted both offences.—He was committed to the Liverpool Quarter Sessions. CONFIDENCE IN HIS INNOCENCE —Richard Bradford, 71, Catherine-street was charged with having stolen by finding, a pocket containing 16s. 6d. the property of Jessie Birchall, 54, Catherine-street.—Prisoner pleaded not guilty.—When the case was called on, the prisoner was informed that his wife had consulted a local solicitor with reference to his defence and it was suggested, in his interests that the case should be adjourned until the following day.—Prisoner said he did not know that be required anyone to defend him and he much preferred that the case should proceed. He maintained this attitude, even after his wife had suggested that it would be better to obtain an adjourn- ment.—lt was decided to proceed with the case.—Chief Inspector Williamson said that the previous afternoon about 2.50 Mrs Birchen was going along Lilford-stre et towards 13ewsey-road. Fastened round her waist was a market pocket in which there was His. 6d. and a small book. When she was nearing Bewsey Bridge she discovered that the pocket was missing. Turning round she saw the prisoner with his wife and child approaching in a cart. Immediately she missed the pocket she turned round and the defend- ant was then standing by his horse's head at the corner of Lilford-street counting some money. She was going towards him to make inquiries concerning the pocket, but the prisoner turned round and immediately drove away. She went a short distance and met Mrs Leather of Oliver-street, who said she had found the pocket but there was nothing in it except the book. Another woman would say that she was in Lilford-street, and saw the pocket lying on the ground, rod her little boy touched it with his foot and said there was some money in it. He was just going to pick it up when the defend- ant drove up, picked up the pocket and handed it to his wife. Prisoner was seen by Sergeant Holland and he stated that he got out of his cart to fasten something on the harness. He admitted picking the pocket up, hut there being nothing in it, he threw it away again.— Several witnesses were called and prisoner stoutly denied having taken the money, but admitted picking up the pocket and throwing it away again.—The magistrates said there was a doubt in the case and prisoner would be given the benefit of it.—The case war dismissed'

THEFTS OF HortsE-Ruos.—Thomas Spender, 9, Williamson-square. was charged on remand with steal- ing from the stables of Messrs R A Naylor Ltd a horse rug, value ss, and further charged with stealing during 1910, from a yaid in Knutsford-road, a horse-rug value ' ss, the property of John Lunt. Chief Inspector Williamson said the defendant was a carter in the employ of Messrs it A Naylor Ltd, of Howley, and it came to the knowledge of the police that the defendant had at his house a small quantity of new timber and some horse cloths. Defendant was seen by Detec- tive Sergeant Holland and asked if he could account for having a horse cloth in his possession. He said it was the summer cloth, and they were supposed to take it home and look after it. Messrs Naylor said the man had no right to take cloths home, they were supposed to hand them in at the end of the season. THEFTS BY WOMEN.—Ada Duncalf, ;Barlow's Cottages, Lower Walton, and Lily James, Acker's Pits Latchford Without, appeared in answer to two charges of thefts from tradesmen in the town and Drincalf was further charged with stealing boots and handkerchiefs to the value of £l. 14s, the property of EE V Morton and others. Chief Inspector Williamson said the two prisoners were sisters, Duncalf being a married woman. For some considerable time past there had

been a series of thefts taking place from pawnshops. The two women had been seen in the shops with a bassinette and a baby. They had priced goods and suspicion had been attached to them. on Friday last Sergeant Reddington heard that Duncalf had pledged some silk handkerchiefs at Stubbs Limited and on going to the shop in the afternoon he found the woman in the shop. As he went into the shop the prisoner James went down the road with the bassinette in which were found a pair of boots and a bonnett. The boots had been stolen from Stubbs and the bonnett from another shop. Other thefts were traced to them and the point he wished to emphasise was that the thefts must have been going on for a con- siderable time. The prisoners were each fined £2 on the first charge or a month's imprisonment. On the other two charges they were committed to prison for one day.

THEFTS FROM HOME.—Minnie Nixdorf (15), 13, Farrell-street was charr=ed with stealing during the past six months a blanket, bed-sheet and table cloth value Bs. the property of her father, Gustav Nixdorf—Chief Inspector Williamson said the girls father had missed goods from the house during the last six months. When brought to the police station she admitted taking the goods. She had got. the articles pawned and shared the money with a friend of hers.—She was sent to the Devon and Exeter reformatory until she attains the age of 19 and the father was ordered to pay 2s. per week towards her maintenance.

The magis- trates dismissed the summonses, each side to pay their own costs.

ST. HELENS INTERVIEWS TWO YEARS AGO AND TO-DAY.

The following brief account of an interview with a St. Helens woman two years ago, and its sequel, will be read with keen interest by every citizen. Yearly two years ago, Mrs. S. Jackson, of 52, Albert- street, close to the Infants' School, St. Helens, said : " Some years ago I was terribly had with dropsy. The trouble come on just before a confinement and continued afterwards. It was all due to my kidneys being in such a disordered state. First of all I was taken with awful pains in the lower part of the back. I became nearly crippled with them, and many a time I have had great trouble to get out of bed, for I could hardly straighten myself. The pains were at their worst first thing in the mornings. Then my limbs and ' body gradually filled with water, until I had become about twice my usual size.

"There was a scalding pain when passing the, urinary excretions ; they were discoloured, and con- tained a dark, heavykdiment. I got no better while I was having medical treatment, but one day I picked up a booklet, and read about a woman who had been cured by Doan's Backache Kidney Pills, and began to use them.

" By the time I had used two boxes I could tell that I had got the right medicine. With every box I got better still, and I can truly say that I have derived wonderful benefit from the medicine. I am now feel- ing in better health than I have done for years, and it is all due to Doan's pills. I shall always be grateful for what they have done for me. (Signed), Mrs. S. Jackson."

Nearly two years later, Mrs. Jackson says :--`• I am pleased to say that I am keeping in good health, thanks to Doan's Backache Kidney Pills. I would not be without a box in the house for anything." WINTER DIET.

In cold weather it is absolutely necessary to give careful attention to our daily diet. To combat successfully the troubles that winter brings we must be well nourished. Foods that merely fill the stomach and appease the appetite are not sufficient What we require and must have is pure, sound and efficacious nourishment. Some of the moat valuable foods are the cheapest so far as money is concerned. The most striking example of highly-nutritious concentrated food that can always be bought at a low price is the Greek Currant. This little dried grape is a veritable store-house of nutriment, and just the ideal food required at all times, especially in winter. It is merely a case of Nature craving for Nature; because to feel hungry is natural, and to satisfy it with Nature's own pure product is strictly in keeping with the fitness of things. Currants contain more than 80 per cent. pure nutriment —all put there by Nature. Quite immaterial is the form in which Currants are taken. So long as they appear in liberal quantities the body will be soundly nourished. An entirely new and interesting little book telling you all about Currants, and giving recipes for a great variety of sweet dishes which can be made with Currants, has just been published, and your Grocer will give you a free copy on request. The book is called "Home Recipes for Cooking Currants."

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

EX-POLICE SEPERINTENDEN rs DE k PH.

The death has taken place, at Birmingham, of ex-Superintendnot Leah, for many years one of the best-known officers of the Cheshire constabulary. Superintendent Leah served with the Cheshire force thirty-seven years, several times distinguished himself in criminal cases, and retired eleven years ago. He was int charge at various times of the districts of Crewe, Nantwich, and Runcorn. WIDNES LIBERALS.

MR. 3.IARTINDALE AND HIS CANDIDATURE. There was an enthusiastic gathering of the leading Liberal workers in the Widnes Division at the Widnes Liberal Club on Monday night, when Mr J B Aitken (president of the association), entertained a company of some seventy to dinner, thus affording an opportunity to meet the prospective candidate, Mr J B hiartindale. Mr Aitken presided and those present included Messrs F J Poole (bon treasurer), W Abercromby (hon secretary), F Burn (secretary of the Lancashire, Cheshire and North-Western Federation), D Lewis (chairman), S Owens, W B Stoddart, C Booth, the Rev A Harvey (chairman of the Leegue of Young Liberals), A A Robertson, Hugh R Rathbone, A E Calvert, T Cosier, J W Towers, J Hurst, J B Leach, J 3 Martindale A Martindale, 3 Bethel! Jones, E Carey, C E Parker, W F Telfer and J Baskerville (Liberal agent for the division). Sir Edward Russell wrote to the Liberal agent for the division :—" I should be glad if you will convey to those present how highly T esteem the qualities of Mr Martindale, and with what good hope I regard the prospects of his winning the seat." After dinner the toast of Mr Martindale's health was proposed by the Chairman. He said the Tory party had hitherto had matters pretty much their own way, but he assured them they were going to have a good run for their money. Mr Martindale was a serious politician. Lie (the Chairman) had not brought them together that night to give them a good time, and he wanted a fair equivalent in return -he asked them to take the question of the next election seriously to mind. Mr Martindale was enthusiastically received on rising to reply. He said Colonel Hall Walker had declared himself in favour of food taxes, and he (the speaker) would see that the voters of the division were not allowed to forget that fact. (Applause.) Colonel Walker had further declared that the record of the Government was a rotten one, and, if he really believed that his years of experience in the House of Commons had taken him strangely out of touch with the masses of the people of the country.

AN OPEN LETTER TO THE STOCKTON HEATH PARISH COUNCIL.

Gentlemen, —I was exceedingly pleased to note your reminder in the local Press a few days ago relating to the condition of the highway adjoining the Swing Bridge. Like the correspondent who suggests you put your house in order by giving a little attention to Alexandra-road, I would remind you of the deplorable condition of Derby-road. The property in this street has been erected more than six years, and yet the street remains unmade. In fact, for six years, the people who inhabit that property have been living alongside a stagnant pool of water. The houses in this street being cottage property, inhabited in the main by people with small families, should receive attention without our coun- cillors having to be reminded of the fact. I venture to assert that a similar number of families occupying the semi-detached villa type of property would have received attention before now. Not a few doctor's bills and sleepless nights are to be debited to those responsible for their negligence. This however is not the only complaint. The large number of children with no play ground than these filth spots should have some decent place for recreation provided for thorn. Surely this is not a great difficulty. Why should the Council not acquit e some of the land adjoining tbe new echools and convert it into recreation grounds for the children? A few allotment gardens might also with ad‘rantage be provided near the same spot. Unless something of this kind is done we sl•:all witness the enclosure of those healthy Council Schools with property which might convert them into a dungeon. CHARLES DUKES.

===== 0002601_19130222_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
PARR HALL ORGAN.

Sir,— The man that bath no music in himself Nor is not moved with concord of sweet sounds Is fit for tremon, stratagems and spoils. —Shakespear. The case. presented by Mr. Reynolds and others for a new organ in Parr Ball is irrefutable. lam not a muscian myself—though I love music —but I know that in musical circles of the town the present org:in is spoken of with scorn and der:sion and has become quite a byeword. I once heard a leading organist say some words to this effect " Tnat he wouldn't soil his fingers on it &c., &c." As Warrington of late years,thanks to the persevere- ance of some of its citizens, has become quite famous as a music loving town and is producing some excellent choirs, vocalists and instrumentalists, surely some encout agement and aid should be given in this direction. lam under the impression that very few ratepayers will grudge the cost of a new organ and its upkeep. Personally, what I hope is that when the Corpora- tion do provide a new organ they will see to it that it will be one that be xvell worthy of the town and its musical reputation so that we shall not only have some excellent recitals upon it but that the town will become famous throughout the length and breadth of the land for its Musical Festivals.— Yours faithfully. E. ENGLAND.

Sir,—l feel that Mr Reynolds' letter needs a short reply. Ile writes, of course, as an interested party, being an official of the Musical Society, who wish the ratepayers to buy an organ for Parr Hall so that it may add somewhat to the attractions of their three or four concerts per year. Your other correspondents seem also to be interested in the Society and therefore their desire for the spending ef so much money by the ratepayers must be taken for what it is worth Far be it from me to depreciate the power of good music. I should be foolish to do so, but with the rates at eight shillings in the pound, will the ratepayers approve the spending of so much ,money on an instrument of this size and quality which can oniy be occasionally used, when they realise that even if the Corporation obtain a loan for it—say for 15 years—and the price is only 4.:.1,600, it would cost i',140 per annum for interest and sinking fund alone. Does any person who is not a partisan think for a moment that the public will respond to the call to regular organ recitals when there are other places in the town which offer entertainments more attrac- tive? It is also as well to remember that even recitals cost money, and good men will require good pay. In addition ti which the cleaning and lighting of the Hall are items to be considered—and all this cost for an occasional recital. I ask is it worth the expenditure and the continual charge upon the rates ? Cannot our music lovers get all the organ music they require at the Churches where there are already excellent instruments? At Bold-street Chapel for instance the well known organist Mr. David Clegg has played more than once, as one of your correspondents has reminded Us. This, I suggest will meet the case, or if there is really need for an instrument such as Mr Reynolds indicates why not open a subscription list and we shall soon find out how readily our friends of the Musical Society will respond. Yours Bcc.. JOHN PEMBERTON.

THE ROYAL VISIT.

THE MAYOR AND THE HONOUR TO WARRINGTON.

Speaking at the annual dinner of the, Warrington and District Grocers' and Pro- vision Dealers' Association on Thursday evening, the Mayor referred to the forth- coming visit of their Majesties the King and Queen to Warrington in July. He said he was very pleased indeed to think that Warrington would be the first place to be visited by the Royal party on their Lancashire tour. They had also the gratifying anticipation of a much more extended viPw of their Majesties than was possible on the occasion of the visit of the late King Edward Vii. The King and Qaeen would drive through the streets in an opeti carriage. He pre- sumed they would visit the Town Hall and they would afterwards inspect the works of Messrs Joseph Cro-4ield and Sons Ltd. He thought Warrington would be very highly privileged and he only hoped the great event would be favoured with a fine day. (Applause).

Each might have a half, but each wanted the totter half. Mr Forshaw reminded his Honour that Mr Atkinson had tLe benefit of a window in the passage, which was a counter-attraction. Another conference with a view to a settlement took place and Mr Browne suggested that if defendant would agree to plaintiff taking the whole length of the passage to the height of sft. 3i0., defendant could take the whole length above that height. Mr Forshaw suggested 4ft. Gin., as -Mr Browne's suggested height was not suitable for photographs. Mr Browne : 4ft. Gin, would be no use to us.

His Honour intimated that he would adjourn the case till next Court day to give the parties an oppor- tunity of discussing the point at isque with the reminder that which ever way he might decide (if they did not agree) Mr Thornton might lt,se a good tenant. It was a curious case.

Many people fly to dangerous drugs, such as morphia, etc., when affected by headache, sleepless- ness and other nervous disorders. Doctors are unanimous in deploring the use of these drugs, and advise a simple, safe home treatment. One of the best remedies can be made up by any chemist, and it -is wonderfully effective in toning up the nerves and removing depression. Ilere is the pt escription :-1 oz. of Alvexo (be sure you get this 'ngredient right), 1 oz. of Glycerine loz. of Tincture of Gentian. Add water to made up six ozs. The dose is a teaspoonful three or four times a day, in water if preferred. In cases of severe nerve pains (such as neuralgia) take a dose hourly until relieved. This simple mixture will cure the worst headache, remove depression and "that tired feeling " and give the nervous system a wonderful " fitness."

COUNCILLOR'S SILVER WEDDING CELEBRATION.

P o MRS. HENSITALL.

!rkin•on.

accommcdata the ever-increasing congregations. I has therefore been decided to aril the preset t premises and to build a new church aid Sunday .chool on Inc Knutsford-road, a much more coriver,ient position. The estimated coatis about ;4:2,100. Mr Henshall has been able to g.ther round him a very willing hand of workers and before lo g they hope to make a practical beginning with their new scheme. From time to time sociAl evenings and concerts have been given by members of the church, and by this means substantial funds have gradually accummulated. To further augment the fundi4 Mr and Mrs Ilenshall have invited about 300 members and friends to their silver weddirg celebration. This unique gathering will be held to-day in Friars Green Sundry School. Mr and Mrs Henshall have asLed all their friends to discontinue the usual practice of giving presents arid instead of ,thist to make a small contribution in aid of the good work at Grappenhall. A social gathering and concert will be held at night. We must ask our readers to peruse our next isssue for the result. Meanwhile we wish Councillor and Mrs Henshall con- tinued health and prosperity, and happiness and success in their work.

scheme which was going to treat men as partly criminal They spoke of the vagrant unemployed as if they were resporsible for that position in which they found them- selves. Men Ware unemployed hecanse workers were working at a higher intensity of labour and not getting enough wages. Unemployment must exist so long as they bad a wages system. The only thing to provide It solution of the unemployment question %sea a reduction in the hours of labour—(hear, hear)—and there was nothing in the corstitntion ci Labour Exchanges which would bring that about. They had increased the mobility of labooir because the employers desired It Without Labour Exchanges employers had to compete. for labour hut now the work.rs were competing amongst themselves for jobs. The employers bad always objected to competing for labour knowing, as they did, that it gave an independence to the worker. Under the Labour Exchanges men were registered as though they were so many bales of cotton going through the Cottsn Exchange. When talking of unemployment they were talking of something touching the vitals of human society. They bad got something they had neither asked for nor desired and they had got to have it whether they wanted it or not. (Laughter.) Claim- ing that the only basis of treatment, and at nay rate the only human basis was that of equity,he was entirely opposed to a scheme whereby workers should he gold as chattel sltves at the dictates of those who were wrongly termed employers. (Applause). Mr E NVYNsa: Joslts (L Chester), spoke strongly in favour of the Exchanges and said the previous speaker had ussd the usual ling) of the Socialist speakers. The worker had the solution of the problem in his own hands. (Hear, bear.) If the workers of tbe country had sufficient commonsense to combine tneether they could get anything they required and no Government could stop them. (Socialist voices: " Come over.") No one Act of Parliament could solve the unemployment question ; it would never he solved until the Govern- ment of the day made up their minds to solve it. The Labour Excharges bad done a great deal of good work and the Liberal Party had done more for the benefit of the people of the country than ever had been done and they would still continue to pass legislation for the benefit of the workers of the country. They were making up their minds to deal seriously with the great problem of unemployment—a problem which should be lifted above party politics and in the solving of which all parties should be united, (Applause). Mr CHAMBERLAIN (SOC., Gerton), said the member for Chester had not contributed ene iota to the subject under discussion. In the first place he told them the workers had the power to solve the question and then afterwards atated that the Government only had the power to find a solution of the unemployed problem. Labent exchanges were out to smash trade unions. By Labour exchanges they bad set up a parasitical ariny and iscreased the number of parasites on the backs of the workers at the presest day. They bad been set up with the object of carrying on the competitive system whereby the working classes could he exploited to give security to the capitalist classes. (Applause). r JoNES spoke in favour of the resolution and said that if the Socialists said they were going to reduce the hours of labour indefinitel i they might as well say the solution of the unemployment question was not to do any work at all. (SocrausT VoICH : "The House of Lords have managed it.")

, The PRIME MINISTER (Nlr E A White) supporoted the resolution, and said he could see no reason why trade unions should n,-,t have organised and formed their own Exchanges. If they bad done so he would have preferred that form to the present system. The Labour Exchanges were however, capable of doing a good and useful work. (Applause). Mr JARMAN (the leader of the Opposition) replied to the Socialist arguments, and said they could mane Labour Exchanges a decided success by all combining together. If the thing was fairly and squarely worked without political prejudice they wonld get much better work done than they were doing at wesent. Mr J HELSBY (Soc., Colne Valley) thought the debate had been confined to a trial of the Socialist Party, and not a discussion on Labour Exchanges. Mr WILLIAMS in reply argued that Labour Exchanges were a necessity under present industrial conditions. It was more dignified to apply at the Labour Exchange than to have to scramble and tout for a job. (Hear, bear.) They were dealing with present day facts and not with the Utopian ideas advanced by the Socialist Party. They did not prevent unemployment, us such; they were the first link in the chain ; they were there to Introducn order into chaos. (Applause.) _ _ . _ . .

The resolution was carried by's. -liirge majority.

STATUE FOR THS SECONDARY SCHOOL.

The Warrington Secondary School and Technical Institute is to have a statue of Dr J 0( eph Priestly, the great scientist, one of the illustrious band who helped to make Warriugtou Acadarny famous as a seat of learning. At a meeting of the School Management Committee, held on February 13th. Alderman Bennett attended An d explained that a number of gentlemen were pur- chasing a life-sized statue of Dr Joseph Priestly, and that they proposed to offer it to the Education Com- mittee to be placed in a suitable position in the Secondary Schol and Technical Institute. It was resolved that the offer be accepted, and that the best thanks of the committee be given to Alderman Bennett and other subscribers for the gift. At the meeting of the Education Committee on Monday evening Mr Upson said the committee were deeply grateful to the Alder- man for the action he had taken in the matter. The statue was offered to the Museum Com- mittee, hut the members felt that the price was rather prohibitive for their restricted means. Alderman Bennett not only desired a fuller and a richer life and a more beautiful Warrington, but proceeded to act, and this proposal was one of his acts. He had invited a number of gentlemen to join with him in the pur- chase of the statue and he was presenting it to the Education Committee. They were all deeply indebted to him as the statue would have such great local interest. (Hear, hear.)

ONE HUNDRED INVENTIONS. STILL A GENIUS AT NEALY 80 YEARS OF AGE.

Mr James Hargreaves, of Sayce-street, Widnes, at the age of 79 years, has just completed over 100 inventions ranging from important chemical processes to that of a novel and efft-itive fire escape, which can he kept in a bedroom. Mr Hargreaves has had a wonderfully dis- tinguished career as a scientist and inventor' and is the survivor of the comparatively small band of earnest men who, perhaps more than any other class, contri- buted to the industrial progress which marked the latter half of the last century.

COUNCILLOR HENSHALL.

Councillor and Mrs Henshall celebrate their silver wedding to-day. Councillor Henshall is a man cf many parts, but above all he is an enthusiastic Methodist. His wife too, is no less an ardent supporter and during their married life they have done yeoman service for the cause. It is becalm) of their love for the church that they have sacrificed a family celebration lor the benefit Giapperlall Inde- pendent Methodist Church Mrs Henshall has been in' the Independent Methodist movement all her life and Mr Benshall since he was 14 years of age. During this period he has filled practically every flier) of the Church. In connection with the Brick-street and Friars' Green Churches he has been Sunday school teacher, Sunday school superintendent, secretary of the church, and also president. The latter office Councillor Henshall held until the year 1904, when he resigned to under- take with Mr Arthur Jolley the resuscitation of Methodism at Grappenhall. When Mr Jol!ey went hack to Friars' Green his place was ably filled by Mr Edward Howard, and in him (as secretary of tne church) Mr Henshall has got a very able lieutenant. LOCAL PARLIAMENT

DISCUSSION ON LABOUR EXCHANGES.

There was a pleasing diversion from the (Winery debates at the weekly meeting of tNe Warrington Parliameutary Debating Society in the Cairo-street Schools on Thursday evening when an open debate was conducted on a reeolution proposed by Mr It Williams, of the ‘Varrington Labour Exchange "that the Labour Exchanges are a necessity for dealing with the problem of unemployment." The room was crowded and the debate proved highly interesting. The chief opposition came from the Socialist benches but on being put to the meeting, the resolution was carried by an over- whelming majority. In proposing the resolution Mr Williams said that he would like to emphasise the fact that unemployment was in no way a party or political matter ; it was one of the greatest stelal evils the whole country had to face. (Hear, hear.) The Labaur Exchange Act passed through the House in 1909 as a non-ccntentious measure. The Poor Law Commission was composed of most eminent persons both employers and workers and the y investigated the problem of unemployment for many )ears. They issued majority and minority reports. Those reports differed in detail but they agreed on one thing, the absolute necessity for Labour Exchanges as the first step in dealing with unemploy- ment. The seoond authority, and probably a more weighty authority, was the Trade Union Congress which passed a unanimous vote in favour of Labour Exchanges. The Unemployed Workmen's Act was passed by the Conservative Perty in 1905. Although the Labour Exchanges came into vogue thrormh the Liberal Government passing the Act he would say that the Conservative Party might claim the credit of hav- ing introduced the idea in the Unemployed Workmen's Act 1905. That Act, amongst other things, was pro- moted to supply work to men who became unemployed through no fault of their own. But work, as work in itself, was no cure for unemployment. By that Unemployed Workmen's Act, work was provided at great cost; there was a lot of useless work done to provide men with jobs. What they were wanting to-day was prevention of unemployment, not palliation (Hear, hear.) When they provi.ded work for men who were unemployed without iemoving the factors which caused unemployment, it was putting the cart before the horse. They were looking without a light in a dark room for a black cat that was not there. (Laughter.) They should first of all attack the defects which caused unemployment. Referring to the various kinds of unemployment the speaker said first of all they had cyclical depression, unemployment which was caused he trade depression. They had the failure of a cotton crop, a famine in India, a failure of the corn crop or something of that description, which sent a wave of depression over the country. Then there was season- able unemployment, those who were Imlay in summer but slack iu winter. Thirdly, they had ordirary unemployment; the man who was temporarily out of work or out of work because he happened to be in a trade which dis not require workmen at the time. There were a large number of trades overstocked with men and large numbers of trades in which there were not sufficient workmen. Lack of mobility was another cause of unemployment, consequently there were jobs vacant and men not there to get them. The fifth cause was defects in character—idle, vicious, drinking, and things of that description. The last cause, and most difficult to deal with, was unemployment due to mortal labourers. In the first place it was a good thing when they were going to tackle a problem to know what it was. No-one really knew what unemployment was. The only way by which they could collect statistics was registration and for that they must have a universal system of Labour Exchanges. (Hear, hear.) They had an Exchange for every commodity under the sun and yet, labour, without which no commodity was worth a farthing, had no exchange at all. Was it not ccmmon- sense to provide men with an Exchange, and instead of wasting employer's and workmen's time give the work- men a place where they could go, knowing that if there was a job available he could get it and where the employer could go, knowing that if there was an unem- ployed workman he could get him. (Hear, bear.) In the first two causes of unemployment, cyclical and seasonal. they could only play a small part. They must have a reserve of labeur. They centralised men and moved them when they were not wanted, cad brought them back when they wera wanted. Dealing with the Mr Williams said they work and thousands Of ho ar ddi nt 11 or yuskainnddso fo fu nme neinp I ou uy eonft, jobs going begging, and the object of the Labour Exchanges was to bring both together. Employers were finding out through the Labour Exchanges where there were workers, so that they could build factories. Here, in Warrington, a large Manchester firm were going to build a factory because they were satisfied through the Labour Exchange that there were sufficient men to work here. (Applause.) If the Lit our Exchange acted properly it would bring the hest workers to the top, and the worst classes would rink. At some length the speaker illustrated the various points in favour of the Labour Exchanges, and said the cure for the casual labourer difficulty was to organis the casual labour market so that employers might draw on a common fund of labour. the Labour Exchanges had established a system whereby they had stopped the entry • into dock labouring until the reserve had got down to the necessary dimensions. They had locked up the whole port of Liverpool by complicated machinery. Labour Exchanges were an absolute necessity. They must find out the demand for and supply of labour and co-ordinate them together and deal with cyclical and seasonal unemployment and the problem of the casual labourer. During the last year the Labour Exchanges had placed 785,239 persons (160,428 temporary), 266,622 casual jobs and the number of applications received was 2,423,213. Unemployment did not always mean distress; a man was unemployed when he was passing from one job to another. l'heir register was always swollen with a larger number of men than they could provide jobs for, simply because those men were in transition from one job to another. They must have universal support. ihey had paid for Labour Exchanges, they had got to have them, and they might as well use them. (Laughter) Every worker unemployed was a drag on the wheels of industry. Anything that could be done to mitigate unemploymeut should appeal to every person. Labour exchanges were the first and most necessary step in deeling with unemployment. They were not a cure for unemployment, they did not provide work, but they were the first step towards the solution of unemploy- ment. (Applause). Mr TATTERSALL formerly seconded the resolution. Mr C DCKES (SOC., West Ham), led the opposition and in the first place denied that the Labour Exchanges were a necessity for the solution of the unemployment question. It was wrong to say that the unemployed army was necessary to meet the fluctuations' of trade. The whole scheme was merely one cog in the wheel of segregation of the unfit. They were asked to support a

The Examiner; Saturday, Februar 22, 1913.

THE LATE MISS MASSY.

Funeral at Grappenhall Church.

One who was greatly respected, not only for her good work, but for charm of personality, passed away on Sunday evening in the person of Miss Emily Jane Massy, of Green Bank, Latchford. The news of her death came quite unexpectedly, as comparatively few were aware that she was seriously ill. She had been suffering from influenza,which,lrowever,was succeeded by complications. So short was her illness, that she had arranged for the annual sale of work in cln- nection with the Brabazon Employment Scheme to be held at her residence on the 13th inst. That arrange- ment had to be cancelled and the sale was conducted at The Hollies, the residence of Mrs Broadbent The deceased lady was the daughter of the late Mr Hugh Massy, M.R.O S., and Sarah Shoobert Massy, of Bath, and on the death of her father went with her mother to Hough Green, Chester, .where she resided for some years. She left Chester for Warrington about 21 years ago. The greater part of her life was devoted to assisting local charitable and philanthropic institu- tions. She was prominently identified with the Infirm- ary,of which she was a life governor. Other institutions which had her practical support were the Warrington and District Nursing Association, the Church of England Waifs and Strays Society, the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children, the Clergy Daughters' School, and local Church of England sales of work. She was one of the first associates of the West Cheshire Needlework Guild, and the Warrington Needlework Guild and on the com- mencement of the Rod Cross Society she became one of the first township leaders of the district. She was also an ardent supporter of the National Service League. A staunch member of the Church of En'gland, Miss Massy regularly attended the services held in Grappenhall Parish Church, and rendered valuable assistance to all the parochial organizations. She took a keen interest in the neighbouring parish of Christ Church, Latchford, and was present at almost all the sales of work. _

Of a generous disposition, Miss Massey, not only subscribed to local institutions, but to many outside the district, notably to the Liverpool Cathedral fund. She always as.isted those in need and her death leaves a gap which it will be difficult to fill. THE FUNERAL.

On Thursday afternr.on the funeral took place in Grappenhall Churchyard. There was a service in the Church, at which the officiating clergy were the Rev Canon Willis (who also performed the last rites at the graveside), the Rev H V Pigot (Rector of Grappenball) and the Rev Horace Stephens (Rector at Eq.. Paul's, Chorlton-on-Medlock), brother-in-law of the deceased The choir was in attendance, and the hymns "Jesus lives " and "On the Resurrection M,,rning " along with the 00th Psalm were sung. Mr W A Woods was at the organ and played "0 Rest in the Lord " and the Dead March in " Saul." At the graveside the choir sang the hymn " Now the labourer's task is o'er." he chief mourners were :—Mr Massy, Mrs Stephens, Mr and Mrs Sainsbury, Mr and Miss Rose, Dr Horace Stephens, Mr James Stephens, Dr Langdale, Mr and Mrs G Dakin, Nurse Callender, four indoor servants and three outdoor servants. A closed motor car wcs sent by Mr and Mrs H P Densham and Mr and Mrs A S Bleckly. Amongst others present were: The Misses Broadbent, Miss Mary Gomel), Miss Coxon, Mr and Mrs Vere B Davies, Mrs r Ridgway, Mr and Mrs William Tinsley, Miss May Fairclough, Mrs Ernest Atkin, Inspactor Wainwright (N.S.P.C.C.),Miss G rdner Mrs Willis, Mrs F A Frost, the Revs Horace Gray, G C Briggs and E C E Carleton, Mr A Lister-Kaye and Major Appleton. A large number of floral tributes were sent by relatives and fri3nds.

LORD ROSEBERY ON CAPTAIN SCOTT.

Speaking on Wednesday at the annual meeting of the London Topographical Society, Lord Rosebery said among those who needed no memorial were the men whose bones had just been found in the Antarctic. The appeal of Captain Scott would be as lasting a memorial as a statue or memorial.

NEXT WEEK AT THE HIPPODROME.

A GRAPHIC PORTRAYAL _OF THE " TITANTIC " DISASTER.

On Monday the public are promised something in the way of a stage product at the Hippodrome that bids fair to be called unique. This is the full story of the brief and tragic voyage of the "Titanic," presented with the aid of every conceivable stage illusion and effect. Let it be said at once that the cinematograph has no part in this The Poole's of Myriorama fame have for many years been identified with scenic wonders, but in this achievement they have surpassed anything previously effected. A brief resume will give some idea of the sequence of the various incidents. Starting with the departure of the " Titanic " from Southampton, she is next seen off Queenstown, then to the open. This is truly a credit to the ingenuity of scenic artist and electrician. It is the thing itself. Portholes ablaze with light, and smoke pouring from her funnels, the good ship proudly steaming the seas on her maiden voyage. The French ship " uraine " sounds the firt.t note of danger: "Look out for ice." The disaster follows;. another scene of grim realism. In addition to the above there will be a strong variety programme including The Gallimore Trio in. a great comedy act. •

Local Members' Records. The fifth break in the present Parlirimentary session occurred on Saturday, when the House adjourned until March 6th. The session, which opened in February of last year, has been prolific of divisions, although the record of 648 set up in 1902 has not been reached. Of the total of 604 recorded during the session, no fewer than 134 were since the Christmas recess.,—The following shows the number of votes given during the sesqioli by the members in Warring- ton anri district :—Flarold Smith, Warrington (C), 382; U Barnston, Edisbury (C), 451 ; J F L Brunner, Northwich (L). 303; Rigby Swift, St Helens (0), 300; Colonel A Hall Walker; Widnes (0), 134; Walsh, Ince (Lab.), 396 ; Viscount Wolmer, Newton (320).

LORDS AND THE RAILWAY BILL. The House of Lords on Wednesday rejected the clause in the Railways (No 2) Bill which fixes a five years' time limit. The Bill, therefore, goes back to the Commons in the form in which the railway directors desire it, and presumably the battle between the Government and the companies will be resumed in the House of Commons two or three weeks hence.

LORD ROSEBERY ON CAPTAIN SCOTT. Speaking on Wednesday at the annual meeting of the Loudon Topographical Society, Lord Rosebery said among those who needed no memorial were the men whose bones had just been found in the Antarctic. The appeal of Captain Scott would be as lasting a memorial as a statue or memorial.

MOORE. WESLEY GUILD.—The members of the Guild met on Wednesday evening at a meeting to be addressed by Miss Ball, who is the leader of the Women's Circle in Warrington. Miss Ball based her remarks for the most part on the title of a small book, namely " Little is much if God is in it." She told in a very interesting way of many experiences of her own, where soldiers rind many others had been brought into the fold of God Miss Dutton presi.led and a very profitable tune was Spent. HOME MISSIONARY MEETING.—On Thursday evening the Home Missionary meeting was held, presided over by Mr Murphy, of Penketh, who made a very able chairman. The Rev H aliddlewood gave a splendid address full of encouragement, and appealed to his hearers to help the cause all they could by giving. The collections show a fair increase en last year. ORFORD• PRESENTATION TO Mn T COREETT.—A hot-pot supper and entertainment, given by the firm of Messrs Mortimer and Co., Orford Tannery, was held in the Assembly Rooms, Sankey-street, on Thursday evening, the 13th inst. The occasion was the presenting to Mr 1' Corbett of an easy chair from the firm, and a mahogany timepiece and case of pipes from the work- men, on his retiring from the position of head tanner. —ln making the presentation, Mr Mortimer said Mr . Corbett had for 11.1 years been a good and faithful servant at Orford Tannery, and he hoped that he would be spared to enjoy his rest for many years to come. Mr J Ingham made the presentation on behalf of the employees. He said that they hau always honoured Mr Corbett, and always would do, for the example he had sat them. He haa climbed to the top of the ladder, and had gained a well-earned rest. Mr Corbett, in reply, said be did not know how to thank them for the beautiful and useful presents which they had given him. they would remind him of the many kininesses which had been shown him by his old firm. He had always tried to do his duty equally to master and man, and if there had been any short comings or differences, he hoped they would forgive him. A few of the company obliged with songs, &c. STOCKTON HEATH. NURSING ASSoCIATION.—Tho invaluable work which is being done by the Stockton Heath and District Nursing Association was under review at the sixth annual meeting held in the Church schools on Tuesday evening, under the presidency of Mr Vera B Davies The total number of cases visited during 1912 was 145, viz., Medical cases, 43, 715 visits ; and surgical cases, including la operations, 102, 1,781 visits. Of the patients, 122 were convalescent or cured. Seventeen were dead, four were transferred to hospital and two remained on the books. The efficient services of Nurse Wherritt had been greatly appreciated. Two recom- mendations had been issued to convalescent homes during the year.—The President said tbe report was a very satisfactory one. No fewer than 2,496 cases had been visited during the year, showing that the nurse must have worked very hard. He had beard the nurse spoken of very highly on all hands. (Applause) the report of the hon treasurer (Mr J Bouchier) showed that the subscriptions for the year amounted to £96 15s 51d. The total expenses, apart from the nurse's salary, were £l2 14s 2d, including .£2 to the Rhyl Convalescent Institution. The result was very satisfactory, and the Ladies' Committee were thanked for the very active interest they took in regard to financial matters. The total balance at the bankers amounted to £B2 183 9d. Ihe report and the accounts were adopted on the motion of the Rev J Collins, seconded by Mr J Longland. The committees were constituted as follows :—Committee ,of Management : Mesdames V B Davies, Henry Greenall, Bradshaw, Milling, Picton, Grocott, Silcock, Baxter, Miss Seward, the Rev G Curtis and Messrs William Bainbridge, Thomas Baxter, H Clarke, C W Davenport, J Grocott. E J Hall, F A Harris, T J Lee, J Longland, J alcDermid, E Pearson, F Peudlebury, J A Ransome, S P Silcock, F Timperley L A Voisey, E Wallington, H J Westbrook, and W H Woodcock. Executive Committee.—Mrs V B Davies, Mrs Greenall, Mrs Milling, Mrs Silcock, Mr 1' Baxter, Mr V B Davies, Mr T J Lee, Mr J Longland, Mr 14' Tirnperley, Mr W Richardson, Mr W H Woodcock, Mr J NV Bouchier (hon. treasurer), and Mr Edward Brad- shaw (hon. secretary). Mr Vete B Davies, whose services on bshalf of the association were highly praised was re-elected president.—The President proposed a vote of thanks to the Ladies Committee for their services, with special thanks to Mrs Milling.—Mr Woodcock seconded, and said the number of cases was very ranch less than it was last year, which pointed to the general health of the district.—The Vicar said the good health of the district was accounted for by the cool surnmer.—Mr Woodcock: And that operated in Warrington.—The motion was zarried. Longland moved a vote of thanks to the officers and the nurse for their services. The Vicar seconded, and this motion was also heartily agreed to.
